Addendum 3. Additional information related to risk factors and mitigation measures for Section 
G.2. 
Risk Factor 1: 
Component 1 puts a significant emphasis on the strengthening of the coordination between relevant 
government bodies and increased participation of local communities (Pasture Users Unions and other local 
member based organizations). The Funding Proposal builds on lessons learned from previous 
experiences. For example, in 2013, there was an attempt to build cooperation between the SAEPF 
(responsible for forests) and the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Industry (responsible for pasture). The 
cooperation aimed to tackle the problem of pasture ecosystems management in an integrated manner. 
The memorandum had set a condition for Pasture Committees (PCs) to rent pasture lands from the leskhoz 
for three to five years; while this was an improvement, the initiative did not produce all expected results as 
it lacked follow-up support and implementation on the ground (details in Annex 9, WP “Climate change and 
ecosystem-based NRM”).  
Moreover, in 2017, a group of national NGOs and international organization prepared some amendments 
to the State Forest Fund regulation on use of grazing land proposing joint planning on the use of pasture 
by Pasture Committees and Forest institutions. The draft was submitted to the Government Office for 
review but it needs to be followed up. At the time of Project design (2017-2018), this represented a 
substantial momentum showing the willingness and preparedness of civil societies and their 
representatives to start harmonizing the discrepant regulations of State Forest Fund (which includes also 
land used for grazing) and State Land Fund (which includes rangeland), which the project will promote 
through evidence based policy dialogue under Component 1.   
Additionally, in the context of the GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support programme, also implemented 
by FAO, the NDA, in consultation with relevant stakeholders, including local communities, and other 
government institutions is raising awareness and conducting discussions at different levels on the role of 
the GCF in transformational change in the Kyrgyz Republic. Important efforts are devoted under the first 
readiness grant to ensure coordination and country ownership of GCF investments. Additionally, FAO has 
allocated own resources to work specifically with communities where FAO and WFP GCF projects are 
expected to be implemented to prepare the ground for successful implementation of these large-scale 
investments.  
 
Coordination amongst all the key stakeholders will be ensured through the Project Steering Committee, 
which will include: SAEPF, Climate Finance Centre (CFC), ARIS, the Russian Kyrgyz Development Fund 
(RKDF), the Ministry of Agriculture, Food Industries and Melioration (MAFIM), the Pasture Department 
(under MAFIM), State Agency for Local Self Government and Interethnic Relations (SALSGIER), the 
Ministry of Emergency Situations (MES), Kyrgyz Hydromet (under MES), the State Registration Agency, 
and the FAO Representation in Kyrgyzstan (cf. Annex 2 Chapter 5, Section A. A. Implementation 
principles and governance(page 146)). Additionally, the establishment of INRMCRP coordination and 
transversal sharing of information (already agreed during the national engagement process (cf. Annex 5, 
ESMF, Section 6.2 stakeholder engagement  (pages 41-45)) will be mainstreamed at the local level to 
ensure participation of local communities and local institutions in the management of NRM. This approach 
has already been proved effective in both Kyrgyzstan (cf. Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Chapter 3, Section II. 
Recent portfolio and performance (page 77)) and other countries such as Armenia and Lebanon where 
FAO is promoting such approach with positive results. 
 
The Pasture Law of 2009 transferred the management of pastures from the State Land Fund (SLF) to the 
local governments, which in turn can further delegate management to the Pasture Users’ Unions (PUUs).

=== PAGE 96 ===
ANNEXES 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 90 OF 81 
 
 
 
 
I 
So far, all 471 Aiyl Okmotu (local government of the Aiyl Aimak) transferred pasture management to 
established PUUs (Annex 9, Working Paper “WP - NRM Policy and Governance”). The Pasture Law covers 
the following topics: pasture management and use planning and implementation, monitoring of pasture 
conditions, defining and collecting pasture fees, pasture-use-related conflict management, and managing 
funds collected from pasture use for pasture improvement and management.  
The support to the implementation of the Pasture law is key and mainstreamed throughout the community 
mobilization (Activity 1.3.1 under Component 1). The Project will also develop and promote implementation 
of harmonized regulatory frameworks for access to pasture areas (in SLF) and grazing areas on forest land 
(under State Forest Fund (SFF), which is managed by SAEPF), and in the forestry / rangeland investments 
(Component 2 – all activities) (Annex 9, Working Paper “WP - NRM Policy and Governance” for detailed 
analysis; Annex 2 Chapter 1 on previous and ongoing projects on pasture law implementation). 
 
Risk Factor 2: 
The problem of overstocking in Kyrgyzstan is significant, and (as noted in Annex 2, Feasibility Study, 
Chapter 1, Section VI. Sectoral economic performance (page 11)) the number of livestock has 
systematically increased in the recent decades. Increasing animal numbers is often the first strategy for 
farmers to increase incomes. To this end, the Project takes this risk in high consideration, and has 
envisaged a multifold system of incentives from the economic and institutional points of view.  
Regarding rules and regulation, in developing the INRMCRPs under Component 1, the project will support 
the enforcement of existing regulations (Pasture Law, 2009, adopted by the Pasture Department of MAFIM) 
for monitoring pasture condition to assist Pasture Users Union (PUU) in estimating carrying capacity and 
specifying stocking rates in pasture management plans (details also in Annex 9, WP on Pasture 
Management). The Pasture Law of 2009 attributed the management of pasture under State Land Fund to 
the PUUs. Better planning of grazing in terms of spatial management, but also time and animal numbers 
will prevent further overgrazing.  
Additional pastures are available also in State Forest Fund (under management of SAEPF). Under the 
aegis of the Project, the SAEPF will be encouraged to develop grazing land-use protocols and social 
mechanisms that introduce a management arrangement analogous to the PUU/ Pasture Committee 
system (cf. Annex 2, Chapter 4, Section Institutional aspects and implementation arrangements (page 
132)).  
Component 2 will address the problem of low productive livestock by promoting breed-improvements for 
cattle and small ruminants, improved feeding as well improved animal husbandry. Increased productivity 
per head of livestock represents an incentive to limit animal numbers while achieving the same level of 
household income.  
Component 3 will provide significant alternatives to unproductive and unsustainable grazing and livestock 
production practices, by promoting investments in climate sensitive value chains, which as ultimate effects 
will also contribute to income diversification and more sustainable grazing pressure in the Project Area.  
The stakeholders in the project area are aware of the problem generated by overstocking, as expressed 
throughout the consultation process (cf. Annex 5 ESMF, Section V. Structured Consultations (Page 73-
75)). In particular, the stakeholders consulted in participatory meetings throughout the project design 
confirm that poor quality of pastures, danger of natural disasters such as landslides, absence of clean 
water, high number and low quality of livestock, overdependence on livestock production” represent major 
problems for the area. The consulted stakeholders specified also that the project shall include as priorities 
support to the identification of an appropriate number of livestock on pastures, and support the transition 
from quantity to quality of livestock.

ANNEXES

 Addendum 4. Description of monitoring parameters and M&E framework 
The Project management Unit (PMU) will include an M&E unit, which will be in charge for of the 
monitoring/measurement/collection of identified parameters during the eight years of execution. This will 
happen through direct data collection and analysis as well as by reviewing reports from partners that will 
use the same procedure identified by the project. The M&E unit will be supported in this task by FAO 
technical expertise. National institutions will be trained and accompanied in using remote sensing to ensure 
the hand over of knowledge and expertise and continuation after project completion (under Activity 1.2.1). 
Additionally, calculations will be verified at mid-term and termination by an independent team of evaluators. 
Details on functions and structure of the M&E unit are available in Annex 2 Chapter 6, Page 174. 
The PMU will host a M&E Unit, which will conduct data collection and monitoring of Execution Performances, 
coordinate and supervise community monitoring and ground truthing, provide training and technical advice, 
and provide inputs for project reporting and planning. M&E Unit will provide monitoring results of indicators 
for interim and terminal evaluations. 
As detailed in Annex 2 Feasibility Study, Chapter 6 (P.176), M&E Unit will be responsible for ensuring 
Means of Verification (MoV) of indicators listed in the Table 1 below:

The project will look at a series of parameters to monitor and assess forests, orchards and rangelands and 
calculate carbon stocks. These are: 
• 
Land productivity dynamic indicator for each parcel identified and georeferenced (cf. Annex 2 
page 179); 
• 
Forest cover changes >10% via Hansen Forest Cover Change methodology and field 
observations (cf. Annex 2 page 179);  
• 
Hectares with survival rate >65% (cf. Annex 2 page 179).  
Monitoring metrics will also depend from:  
• 
Tree species data according to existing composition of forests in target areas (Spruce forests, 
Walnut forest, Juniper forest, Pistachio forest, Poplar forest and Willows and Gummy forests 
as shrub and riparian forests). References include: Baseline Atlas (Annex 6.b), the Feasibility 
Study (Annex 2, pages 22-23; 31-32: 110-114), and the Working Paper (WP) on Forestry in 
Annex 9. 
• 
Age of trees/Growth rate: for the purposes of the monitoring carbon benefits during the 
project, the age of existing stands in target areas is classified according to Kraft classification 
as reported in the Forest Typology in the Kyrgyz Republic of 2008 as well as in the National 
Forest Assessment carried out by FAO and SAEPF in 2010. (Forestry Working Paper), the 
FAO’s Global Ecological Zones of 2011 and consultation with national and local stakeholders. 
Growth rates of forests in target areas are reported in Annex 2 (pages 225-226). 
• 
Forest cover according to Hansen forest cover of georeferenced plots. 
• 
Land Productivity Dynamics (LPD) of georeferenced plots.

=== PAGE 102 ===
ANNEXES 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 96 OF 81 
 
 
 
 
I 
FAO confirms that the default parameters will be monitored during the project and reported annually. 
Georeferencing of activities and geospatial analysis (Earth Map, introduced in Chapter 6 of the 
Feasibility Study, page 173, as well as in Chapter 1 (page 20, paragraphs 78) and subsequent of the 
FS, and presented in details in Annex 6.b of the submission package) will provide the project with data 
on investments' distribution and improvements against baseline (e.g. LPD, NDVI, Hansen Forest Cover 
Change). Results will be disaggregated per district, community and type of investment. Results will be 
presented on an annual basis and will be included in the Project Annual Report. 
Georeferencing of all investments and using remote sensing combined with ground monitoring (jointly 
with stakeholders) will allow identifying impacts and attributions. The georeferenced archive will 
provide precise indication of where activities are executed (quantitative analysis) and associate to them 
the attributes identified by the baseline (qualitative analysis). This approach will allow the project and 
the AE to follow up via ground-based surveys and remote sensing analysis the evolution of the default 
parameters. 
 
The two main relevant institutions to ensure long-term sustainability of carbon accounting and of other 
geospatial tools are the SAEPF (GCF NDA), the Pasture Department in the Ministry of Agriculture and 
Food Industry (MAFIM) and the Statistic Committee. Knowledge transfer to these institutions will start 
from the initial stage of the project until full handover at the end of the project. The SAEPF, also 
covering the role of NDA, is the institution tasked to report to the UNFCCC. This element represents a 
significant institutional incentive to embed carbon accounting within its functions. FAO has already 
provided stakeholders with a tailored Russian version of both EX-ACT and GLEAM-i to ensure full 
ownership and control of the tool and related processes. For example, SAEPF has already received 
exposure and training to EX-ACT and trainings. Refreshers trainings are budgeted within Activity 1.2.1 
throughout the project duration. This will be coupled with on-the-job learning during project 
implementation. Additionally in addition to the work done during the national engagement process, 
identified stakeholders will be trained on GLEAM-I. MAFIM also showed interest in receiving training 
on GLEAM-i, and a draft Russian version of the tool has been prepared specifically for this project. 
 
Additional opportunity for long term adoption of the relevant monitoring tools and techniques (including 
remote sensing) is provided by the signature in 2016 of the Kyrgyzstan National Spatial Infrastructure 
Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) (cf. Annex 9, WP on Georeferencing Strategy,). This MoU, 
signed among others by SAEPF and MAFIM, aims at the standardization and sharing of national spatial 
data, including in the use of remote sensing for natural resources monitoring, management and 
planning. These signatory institutions will be trained (as part of Activity 1.2.1) to use the appropriate 
remote sensing tools and technologies. The momentum generated by the signature of the 
memorandum constitute a unique occasion to promote further georeferencing among national  
institutions and other development actors, such as the WB, GIZ, IFAD and FAO.  
 
Deforestation/illegal loggings 
 
According to different sources, including 2017 Collect Earth assessment within the framework of the 
FAO implemented project “National Forest Monitoring and Information Systems for a transparent and 
truthful REDD+” (https://www.international-climate-initiative.com/en/details/project/332/), and the 
University of Maryland and the Hansen’s forest cover change dataset results (2000-2018), the 
deforestation rate for Kyrgyzstan ranges from 0 to 0.38% at national level and 0.3% in target areas.  
 
Each of the areas identified for forest investments is georeferenced and accurately analysed in the 
baseline (Baseline Atlas, Annex 6.b). The project will constantly monitor the state of forests in target

=== PAGE 103 ===
ANNEXES 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 97 OF 81 
 
 
 
 
I 
areas via dedicated remote sensing analysis and involvement of communities in the monitoring 
processes as described in Annex 2 Chapter 6 as well as in the georeferencing strategy working paper 
(Annex 9). Furthermore, FAO will identify a control area external to project’s intervention to better 
quantify and follow up on project’s impacts. The identified procedure will allow the project and FAO to 
constantly monitor activities against the baseline and to provide objective and tangible data for carbon 
calculation and independent impact evaluations.  
 
Given the nature and scope of the project and as part of the project’s aim to support the country in 
creating  the enabling environment for an innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource 
planning and monitoring system, deforestation is already part of the project monitoring activity in target 
areas. . Furthermore, the tools developed by FAO (e.g. Collect Earth, Earth Map, EX-ACT, GLEAM-i) 
and practices (e.g. Georeferencing, geospatial analysis and community monitoring) have already been 
introduced to the SAEPF and the Pasture Departments in order to ensure full ownership of the process 
as well as scaling up of this approach at the national level. Finally, local administrations and 
communities will be involved not only in the monitoring of forested areas but also in the using the tools 
and practices introduced by the projects.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP116 
Proposal name: 
Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and 
Rangelands in Kyrgyz Republic (CS-FOR) 
Accredited entity: 
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) 
Country:

Project size:  
Small 
I.
Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1.
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following
remarks:
Strengths 
Points of caution 
GCF grant financing will be used to mobilize 
further financing in forest and rangeland 
sectors creating opportunities for scaling up. 
Coordination and ownership among the 
different partners will be critical for successful 
implementation and to ensure sustainability.  
An enabling environment for climate-related 
green investments will be strengthen and 
capacity-building for all relevant stakeholders 
provided.  
Georeferenced monitoring will be used for the 
evaluation of forest-rangeland ecosystem 
management and will promote evidence-based 
planning informing the business model for 
future replication and scaling up. 
2. 
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and 
conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVII, titled “List of proposed 
conditions and recommendations”.
II. 
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2.1 
Project background 
3.
Kyrgyzstan is a low-income food-deficit country. Livestock is the most important source
of income, the primary source of nutrition, and the financial safety net for the rural poor.
Cropping is limited due to a lack of irrigation infrastructure and worsening climatic conditions –
increasing temperatures and reducing the amount and duration of rainfalls. Climate change
combined with poor management of natural resources have led to overexploitation and
overgrazing by large groups of unproductive livestock, and to increasing forest degradation and
unsustainable harvesting of timber from mountain slopes.
4.
The objective of the proposal is to enhance carbon sequestration and climate resilience
of ecosystems and livelihoods. The project will store 19.8 million tonnes of carbon dioxide
equivalent (tCO2eq) over 20 years. This represents an annual reduction of 990,000 tCO2eq and
7,920,000 tCO2eq over the eight years of project implementation. The total expected reduction

=== PAGE 110 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 110 
 
 
over 20 years represents 7.6 per cent of the country’s total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, 
and 22.6 per cent of the agricultural share of emissions. 
5. 
Aside from the activities at national scale, the project sites are in selected rural 
municipalities represented by the four contiguous districts of Ak-Talaa, Toguz-Toro, Suzak and 
Uzgen.  
6. 
The project seeks a GCF grant of approximatively USD 30 million, or 60 per cent of the 
total project cost. The project is classified as ESS category B. The project management unit costs 
USD 2.5 million, equivalent to 5 per cent of the project cost. This covers staff time, operational 
costs, and travels.  
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis  
Component 1: Evidence-based strengthening of natural resource management governance (total 
cost: USD 5.58; GCF cost: USD 5.18, or 92.8 per cent) 
7. 
This component aims to provide an enabling environment to support investments in 
forest-rangeland management through two approaches: 1) harmonization of relevant 
procedures and regulations for “climate-change sensitive” natural resource management, 
especially for tenure arrangements (output 1.1); and 2) the promotion of evidence-based and 
georeferenced monitoring and evaluation measures for natural resource and forest-rangeland 
ecosystem management (outputs 1.2 and 1.3). It aims to galvanize multi-stakeholder 
partnerships to promote technically sound integrated natural resource management in 
Kyrgyzstan. It also provides measures for capacity and organizational development – creating a 
national expert group for necessary consultations, providing technical support to pasture users 
unions and Leskhozes (local forest institutions) to update and enhance the needed data to 
implement climate-sensitive plans and strategies, and establishing community management 
groups to support work at landscape scale. 
8. 
The preservation of forest-rangeland ecosystems through the prevention of overgrazing 
and overharvesting of the forest/vegetative cover will be more effectively managed with the 
standardization of different legislation and full stakeholder inclusion. Through policy 
harmonization and capacity development at scale, the project will be able to incorporate climate 
risks in current management schemes in forest-rangeland ecosystems. 
Component 2: Green investments for forest and rangeland rehabilitation (total cost: USD 22.40; 
GCF cost: USD 20.30, or 91 per cent) 
9. 
This component aims to contribute to community resilience through capacity 
development of key stakeholders in natural resource management to promote sustainable 
practices like rotational grazing, to enhance monitoring capacities, and to develop and 
implement integrated natural resource management and climate resilience plans (output 2.1). 
This is combined with investments to restore and improve forest-rangelands (output 2.2) in the 
project sites through key interventions: afforestation, reforestation, forest enrichment, pasture 
rehabilitation and livestock investment.  
10. 
Through an integrated approach, stakeholders will be incentivized to include climate 
risks in forest-rangeland planning and management. The skills needed for proper maintenance 
of the investments will be strengthened (e.g. fire protection, pest management and monitoring), 
which will ensure ownership and sustainability post-project.  
Component 3: Climate-sensitive value chain development (total cost: USD 18.01; GCF cost: USD 
3.01, or 16.8 per cent) 
11. 
This component aims to incentivize sustainable investments in carbon sequestration. 
Activities include market assessments, certifications, awareness campaigns on marketing

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 111 
requirements, and other relevant business skills, such as financial literacy to ensure the 
inclusion of smallholder pastoralists and farmers (output 3.1). This is further supported with 
increased access to special credit lines for project-relevant value chains and businesses via the 
Russian Kyrgyz Development Fund (RKDF) co-financing working with local banks to support 
investments with climate mitigation and resilience objectives (output 3.2).  
2.2.1. 
Overall technical assessment 
12.
In summary, the project’s mitigation and adaptation goals are focused on integrated
rangeland and forestry management guided by the prevention of natural resource degradation
and enhancement of ecosystem services; multi-stakeholder engagement with emphasis on
communities and the private sector; promotion of bottom-up activities with local communities;
and the introduction of market-driven incentives for resource users to adopt and maintain
management practices that sequester and preserve stored carbon. All of which will be
supported by an enabling environment with coherent underlying policies and by the
strengthening of key national and local institutions and planning processes, which overall will
improve governance of forest and rangeland in Kyrgyzstan.
13.
There is a strong connectivity between the three components. Components 1 and 2
provide for the creation of an enabling environment, baseline data sets, and capacity combined
with the restoration and rehabilitation of forests and rangelands. Component 3 provides the
scale and sustainability by promoting investments in the natural resources sector in Kyrgyzstan.
The project’s paradigm shift could be adapted and tested in other districts and neighbouring
countries.
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria
3.1 
Impact potential 
   Scale: High 
14.
Under business as usual (substantial degradation trend), the level of forest degradation
would generate an estimated net loss of 1.5 MtCO2eq in 20 years. The project will be able to
avoid the mentioned losses and to generate an additional 18 MtCO2eq sequestration (15.1
MtCO2eq from rangeland and livestock rehabilitation, 2.9 MtCO2eq from forest activities and 0.2
MtCO2eq from other agricultural activities), for a net effect of 19.8 MtCO2eq sequestered.
15.
The project will support the reduction of emissions and enhance carbon storage of about
19.8 MtCO2eq through: (i) the creation of legal and enabling environments supported by an
innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning and monitoring system; (ii)
community-based investments in natural forest regeneration, sustainable forest management,
afforestation and reforestation; (iii) the rehabilitation of rangelands and prevention of further
degradation; (iv) the diversification of options for community livelihoods (i.e. income
portfolios); and (v) the reduction of emission intensity per unit of animal protein (meat and
milk).
16.
Sequestration of 19.8 MtCO2eq in 20 years represents 7.6 per cent of the country’s total
emissions, and 22.6 per cent of the agricultural share of emissions. The cost per tCO2eq is USD
2.5.
17.
Average annual temperatures have increased during the period 1960–2010 (0.025
oC/year) with a higher rate of increase in the period 1990–2010 (0.07 oC/year), with the
greatest rate of warming occurring during the winter months. Annual precipitation has shown a
slight downward trend in the last 20 years (–1.868 mm/year). These small but significant
changes in temperature and rainfall are affecting ecosystems and ecosystem services, and thus
community livelihoods, which depend on natural resources for their living.

=== PAGE 112 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 112 
 
 
18. 
The climate resilience of 432,450 individuals (90,000 households), including 246,497 
women in Leskhozes, national parks, local self-government bodies, community organizations, 
the private sector in the high climate-risk area will be improved, representing 9.8 per cent of the 
country’s total population.  
19. 
As part of a resilience impact measurement analysis methodology, developed by the 
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the resilience capacity index 
has been calculated for the target and control group. For both, a high education level and the 
diversification of income portfolios are the main variables of adaptive capacity. This finding 
confirms the need for economic diversification as part of building resilience. The project will 
increase the resilience of beneficiaries by reducing their dependency on direct uses of natural 
resources, by improving ecosystems functions, and by diversifying livelihoods and income 
sources.  
20. 
An estimated 3,000 ha of dry, high-altitude deforested or severely degraded forest lands 
will be restored through natural forest enrichment practices. In addition, 56,000 ha of forest, 
and 644,000 ha of rangelands will be under improved management. An additional 20 per cent of 
producers will use carbon sequestration technologies and practices.  
21. 
An analysis of the land productivity dynamics via the FAO Earth Map will allow the 
assessment of project’s positive impacts on ecosystems. 
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential     
 
 
 
                  Scale: High 
22. 
The project aims to contribute to shifting national agricultural production from a 
predominantly unsustainable subsistence livestock production impacting on the carbon storage 
of forests and rangelands to a diversified and climate-sensitive value chain business-oriented 
economy where investments in mitigation enhance the resilience of ecosystems and 
communities by providing ecosystem services (e.g. improved climate regulation, flood 
regulation, soil retention and habitat provision). 
23. 
The major constraint on the increase of carbon sequestration in forests and rangelands 
has been the traditional extensive grazing practices and expansion of pasture areas. The project 
aims to provide incentives for communities to preserve and expand forested areas and to better 
manage grazing lands through improved management practices and establishing linkages with 
markets leading to diversified and more profitable agriculture for beneficiaries. 
24. 
As diversification of income sources is demonstrated as the main driver of adaptive 
capacity, the project will reinforce policies, legal frameworks and strategies enabling 
diversification through improved forest and grassland management. Coordination will be 
reinforced among these core planning frameworks and governance structures. The more 
conducive policy and institutional environment will ensure sustainability and facilitate the 
replication and scale up of future investments.  
25. 
Integrated natural resources climate resilient management plans will be developed with 
local governments, pasture committees, communities and the private sector. Improved 
governance is important for private sector investments into afforestation and reforestation.  
26. 
The project through training sessions, engagement workshops, awareness campaigns, 
media outreaches, and knowledge-sharing events will also strengthen knowledge generation 
and dissemination of good practices throughout the country, including to young people. 
Moreover, the tools used, and skills strengthened will ensure that climate change is taken into 
account in future decision-making by the communities, public institutions and private sector. A 
dedicated communication and knowledge management specialist will be assigned to the project 
implementation team.

=== PAGE 113 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 113 
 
 
27. 
The project will inform the national institutional and legal framework by providing 
climate and gender-sensitive evidences and lessons learned, which will provide for long-term 
transformational change. It will also integrate climate risk management into the national and 
subnational planning processes. Moreover, an improved methodology to monitor and assess 
pasture and forest degradation will be developed and used to generate the evidences from 
geospatial data.  
3.3 
Sustainable development potential  
                    Scale: High 
28. 
Community mobilization and training sessions will be undertaken in 50 municipalities 
and will include social mobilization, gender training, institutional support, and the 
establishment of task forces and fire management teams. Women and young people will take 
part in the selection of the project-supported value chains and in the capacity-building activities. 
Access for women to improved natural resources (forests and rangelands) will be encouraged to 
ensure equitable distribution of benefits. The investments under the project will generate up to 
3,300 new jobs. 
29. 
In addition to contributing to carbon sequestration, afforestation/reforestation and 
forest-enrichment activities will improve soil fertility and stability, reduce land degradation, 
improve soil water retention and enrich biodiversity. Plant diversity will also be improved 
under pasture rotation. The expected carbon sequestration will increase the availability of 
drinking water and of non-timber forest products, which were valued in the economic analysis. 
Moreover, climate change mitigation and biodiversity issues will be harmonized across 
strategies and legislation through the planned policy dialogue. 
30. 
Better herd management will contribute to an expected rise of livestock production by 
at least 10 per cent (under the assumption of an increasing share of cows and a decreasing 
livestock herd number by 20 per cent). Individual average financial benefits are positive from 
the selected value chains (internal rates of return between 22 and 39 per cent and net present 
values between USD 5,800 and 21,400 per ha), but the highest benefits are when economic 
benefits are accounted for from ecosystem services. Also, forest users will benefit from 
collecting non-timber forest products.  
3.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
         Scale: High 
31. 
Most of the population lives in the valleys and foothills of the mountains, where 
vulnerability to climate events is particularly high and where around 50–80 per cent of 
agricultural land are affected by land degradation. Food insecurity is exacerbated by climate-
related shocks, including floods and mudslides. Thirteen per cent of children suffer from 
malnutrition, and around 40 per cent of children and women suffer from anaemia due to 
micronutrient deficiencies.  
32. 
Few investments are in the forest sector. Rangeland investments have received more 
attention from donors, but the focus was strictly on livelihoods and not on including carbon 
sequestration, which makes a significant contribution to reducing GHG emissions in the country, 
as explained in the country’s nationally determined contribution. 
3.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
             
      Scale: High 
33. 
The Government of Kyrgyzstan includes ecosystem-based adaptation in its “Climate 
change adaptation programme and action plan for 2015–2017 for the forest and biodiversity 
sector”. Mitigation and adaptation actions in the sector are included in the nationally 
determined contribution. FAO will contribute to the national adaptation planning process by

=== PAGE 114 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 114 
 
 
including forest and rangeland management in the National Adaptation Plan being developed 
with the United Nations Development Programme as GCF readiness partner  
34. 
The project was designed with key governmental agencies, coordination bodies, and 
communities. The main stakeholders identified the project target areas and their roles and 
responsibilities in project implementation. This has created a strong sense of ownership of the 
project, which will contribute to the project performance and sustainability. The private sector 
was also engaged in designing the investments in the key value chains and in the identification 
of target areas and priority expansion areas.  
35. 
Key stakeholders have been part of the project design. In the embodiment of the project 
steering committee, they will continue to be involved in project implementation from planning 
to monitoring. It is expected that the project activities will be included in the government’s 
regular annual work plans. The State Agency for Environment Protection and Forestry (SAEPF), 
the lead governmental body for climate change, acts as the secretariat of the Climate Change 
Coordination Commission (headed by the First Vice-Prime Minister of the Kyrgyz Republic) and 
is one executing entity (EE) in this project, a member of the steering committee and hosting the 
project management unit. Other members of the steering committee include the Ministry of 
Agriculture, Food Industry and Melioration , Community Development and Investment Agency 
(ARIS), RKDF, Climate Finance Center (CFC), Ministry of Emergency Situations (MES), State 
Agency for Local Self Government and Interethnic Relations (SALSGIER), and State Registration 
Agency.  
36. 
The approach for integrated forest and pasture management will be based on a set of 
principles, which are reinforcing country ownership: transparency, participation, 
accountability, fairness/equity, coordination and capacity development. Community 
consultative groups will be established at the local level (watershed or forest enterprise level) 
to participate in the development of integrated natural resources management and climate 
resilient plans. Recommendations for tenure arrangements aiming at improving access and 
sustainable use will be developed.  
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
          Scale: High 
37. 
The level of concessionality is justified by the substantial expected mitigation impact 
coupled with the limited financial return of investment in forests, and the high transaction costs 
associated to mobilizing different stakeholders i.e. government agencies, local self-government 
bodies, Leskhozes, and Pasture Use Unions  for joint investments in rangeland rehabilitation, 
which otherwise would not make the investment attractive for the private sector. 
38. 
Component 3 will ensure the sustainability of the investments in carbon sequestration 
carried out in component 2 and will create economic opportunities to decrease degradation of 
natural resources in the project areas, which will enhance the resilience of target communities. 
All private investments generated under component 3 will be undertaken entirely with private 
funds and not GCF funding, aside from the capacity development support activities. 
39. 
The estimated benefits in terms of sequestration is 19.8 MtCO2eq, with the total 
investment of USD 2.5 per tCO2eq. The project utilizes investment of USD 115 per direct 
beneficiary, and USD 51 per total beneficiary (direct and indirect). The average economic 
benefit per direct beneficiary in the project area is estimated at approximately USD 41.1 per 
year (economic net present value of the operation per beneficiary per year), as a result of the 
project interventions. 
40. 
Overall, GCF grant financing is appropriate for this project given the public nature and 
low financial returns of the GCF-funded investments. Component 1 provides public goods in the 
form of policy and legal framework, monitoring and evaluation systems, and planning and 
monitoring. GCF funds under components 2 and 3 will go toward capacity-building, which

=== PAGE 115 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 115 
 
 
provides broad-based benefits. Additional GCF investments under component 2 will improve 
the management of common pool resources (forests and rangeland), which has a high economic 
return but a low financial return. The revenue-generating activities under component 3 will be 
financed by loans from RKDF and without financing from GCF. 
41. 
This project is co-financed by the Government of Kyrgyzstan at USD 1 million (in kind), 
FAO at USD 0.4 million (in kind), the beneficiaries at USD 3.6 million (in kind) RKDF at USD 15 
million (senior loan). 
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
4.1.1. 
Environmental and social risk category 
42. 
The accredited entity (AE) has carried out an initial risk assessment against its 
environmental and social safeguards and considered the project to be of moderate risk. 
Potential environmental and social risks and impacts identified are not considered major, will 
not extend beyond the area of influence of the project and are neither irreversible nor 
cumulative. The Secretariat confirms the AE’s environmental and social risk category 
assessment, which is equivalent to category B. 
4.1.2. 
Safeguard instrument and information disclosure 
43. 
Since exact subprojects are not determined yet and will be decided during project 
implementation based on demand and consultations with the concerned communities, the AE 
has prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) as the platform to 
guide the identification and management of potential negative environmental and social 
impacts of proposed activities. The ESMF is prepared in compliance with the environmental and 
social management guidelines of the AE, taking into consideration the GCF’s environmental and 
social safeguards requirements. The ESMF will be disclosed on the AE’s and GCF’s websites. 
44. 
The ESMF discussed the legal and institutional framework and provided an overview of 
environmental, climate and social considerations in the country and in the target areas. The 
procedures for identifying and risk-screening procedures for subprojects are also presented, 
including the preparation, approval, and execution and determination of potential impacts and 
mitigation measures. While a detailed environmental and social assessment is not envisaged for 
the activities, all subprojects will be further assessed against the FAO screening criteria for 
environmental and social management. Should the activities require further environmental 
assessment by FAO or by the national regulators, these will be conducted. The FAO 
Environmental and Social Screening checklist will be used to determine if an environmental and 
social management plan is needed for each subproject. The nature, magnitude, reversibility and 
location of impacts are the main elements to consider in the screening of subprojects and will be 
complemented by expert judgement. National environmental impact assessment legislation 
requirements will also be referred to. The project will not fund or be involved in 
subprojects/investments rated as high risk, and it has developed an exclusion list that will not 
be supported by the project. 
4.1.3. 
Key environmental and social risks and impacts and mitigation measures 
45. 
The project is designed to have positive environmental and social outcomes. Key 
interventions on strengthening natural resource management governance, afforestation and 
reforestation activities, and pasture rehabilitation are expected to contribute to climate change

=== PAGE 116 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 116 
 
 
mitigation through increased carbon sequestration and adaptive strategies to enhance climate 
resilience. Potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the activities under the 
project relate to soil conservation; agricultural inputs and fertilizer use; crop residues and solid 
waste; pasture and livestock management; water management; pest and pesticide use and 
management; and ecosystems and biodiversity concerns. One of the investment activities under 
the project includes provision of loans to eligible value chain actors through the RKDF and 
participating commercial banks. These loans may be for packaging equipment, laboratory 
equipment, vacuum and solar driers, agricultural machinery, greenhouses, nurseries for tree 
plantations and commercial orchards, eco-tourism, and agricultural processing facilities, among 
others. The ESMF provides for possible avoidance and mitigation measures to manage the 
identified potential risks and impacts of the subprojects. It also provides for the preparation of a 
detailed environmental and social commitment plan that will incorporate the recommended 
mitigations of the environmental and social assessment as well as the inputs from the 
stakeholder engagement process. The project’s interventions and the corresponding 
management measures will be implemented within the context of the integrated natural 
resource management and climate resilience plans developed through the community 
landscape management groups. The AE confirms that there are no communities over which the 
GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy applies, and the project will not affect cultural heritage. 
Nevertheless, with Kyrgyz pastoralists as a key community stakeholder, the ESMF sets out 
certain safeguards for the communities, including that voluntary agreement by communities for 
any temporary/managed disruption to access to areas of land/natural resources must be 
obtained. Creating synergies with FAO networks, such as the Pastoralist Knowledge Hub, could 
also provide capacity-building opportunities for stakeholders around pastoralist issues. 
4.1.4. 
Institutional arrangements 
46. 
The project will establish a project implementation unit (PIU), which will be responsible 
for the daily management of operations. Among others, a safeguards specialist is expected to be 
based within the PIU. The safeguards specialist is tasked with ensuring overall compliance and 
supporting the safeguard performance monitoring throughout the life of the project including 
all aspects of environmental and social safeguards, grievance redress, stakeholder engagement, 
reporting, coordinating and supervising subproject screening and related environmental and 
social management plan preparation and execution. The project will also provide capacity 
building activities as regards sustainable forest and rangeland management to the involved 
institutions and communities. 
4.1.5. 
Stakeholder engagement, information disclosure and grievance redress 
mechanism 
47. 
Stakeholder consultations with various stakeholders at both national and local levels 
have been conducted during project formulation. It is also planned for stakeholder engagement 
to continue during project implementation through the community landscape management 
groups and various local and national stakeholders and user groups in the development of the 
integrated natural resource management and climate resilience plans. The community 
landscape management groups are informal institutions that, which will be established at the 
local level by the project to advance the participatory management of resources. The AE will 
undertake disclosure for all moderate risk projects and will establish a disclosure portal to 
publicly disclose project documentation, including on environmental and social safeguards. 
Institutional, project-level and internal grievance redress mechanism structures are established 
and time frames to resolve issues and contact details through which to lodge grievances are 
provided. 
4.2 
Gender policy

=== PAGE 117 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 117 
 
 
48. 
The AE has submitted a gender assessment; therefore, it complies with the operational 
guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. The gender assessment describes the 
context of gender issues at the country level, giving reference to the national policy 
environment for promoting gender equality in Kyrgyzstan. It also describes beliefs that shape 
gender roles and perpetuate gender inequality that, which disproportionately affect women. 
Furthermore, the gender assessment provides information on gender issues in the forestry and 
agricultural sectors, such as the limited access to resources for women and their limited 
opportunities to participate in decision-making platforms, including how this affects their needs 
and priorities being ensured.  
49. 
The gender assessment includes general statements on the entry points offered by the 
project to ensure that the needs and priorities of women and men in relation to the project are 
addressed, such as the participation of women in the project and access to benefits. However, it 
is not clear in which specific project activities women will participate and benefit from the 
project. The findings and recommendations of the gender assessment on which the gender 
strategy should be based have not been elaborated.  
50. 
Though stakeholder consultations were undertaken during the preparation of the 
project, it is not clear how the district-level consultations of the target communities themselves 
were used to identify the needs and priorities of women and men in the target communities, 
besides having women included in the community landscape management groups, and how the 
consultation outcomes were used to inform the project design. The AE is advised to continue to 
undertake stakeholder consultations with beneficiary communities to solicit inputs that ensure 
that their needs and priorities are captured in a revised gender action plan and across the 
activities listed therein. Furthermore, the AE recommended assessing the implications in terms 
of social norms and time constraints on women to engage in the various activities that are being 
proposed and how constraints will be addressed.  
51. 
The AE provided a project-level gender action plan with activities that are aligned with 
the project components and outputs. The gender action plan has indicators with sex-
disaggregated targets. For example, 30 per cent of the beneficiary trainees participating in 
capacity-building for agribusiness entrepreneurs will be women. The AE has also provided 
estimated costs for gender-related activities listed in the gender action plan. The baseline has 
been set at zero for activities in the gender action plan. Timelines for implementation are too 
stretched. For example, many activities have a timeline of by end of year 7 and 8, these are too 
stretched for a project that has an implementation period of 8 years. The AE is requested to 
ensure that actions are scheduled for implementation annually, including during the earlier 
years of the project’s implementation, to allow for reporting on an annual basis.  
52. 
Under component 2, the action plan identifies capacity development activities for 
ensuring participation in training and access to benefits from investment activities by both 
women and men in the target local communities. For example, investments that will support 
small-scale nursery establishment, with inputs such as seedlings and drip irrigation, will also 
benefit women in order to contribute to addressing the issue of limited access to resources 
among women. Under component 3, which supports value chain development, training for 
operating agribusinesses and credit lines will be provided. Regarding the latter, the AE is 
recommended to have a target for the women that will access credit lines. The project will 
promote certification of local products, such as non-timber forest products, and there is 
potential for women to benefit from this. The gender assessment also identifies certification of 
local products in the gender action plan as an important source of income and household 
consumption for families living near forests, particularly for female-headed households and 
poor households. The AE is recommended to consider the roles of women and men in accessing 
and using forestry products as discussed in the gender assessment to ensure that project 
activities address their needs and priorities. In addition, the AE is recommended to target 
female-headed households that are identified as vulnerable in the gender assessment by

=== PAGE 118 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 118 
 
 
including, in the gender action plan, targets on actions that will help to address their needs 
within the scope of the project 
53. 
The gender action plan includes arrangements for its implementation, including 
procuring a gender and social development specialist to be part of the project management unit. 
The specialist will be responsible for ensuring the implementation of activities listed in the 
gender action plan.  
54. 
The number of direct and indirect beneficiaries of the project has been disaggregated by 
sex to demonstrate the impact potential of the project in the funding proposal. 
4.3 
Risks 
4.3.1. 
Overall programme assessment (medium)  
 
 
 
 
55. 
GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 29.9 million, which will lead to the 
sequestration of 19.8 MtCO2eq in 20 years by investing in rangeland and forestry management 
in Kyrgyzstan. The Government agencies, the AE and beneficiaries are providing co-financing of 
USD 5 million as in-kind contributions and the RKDF is providing a senior loan of USD 15 
million. 
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the project (medium) 
56. 
FAO will serve both as the AE and co-EE for the project. The AE has funded over USD 
600 million for projects supporting countries in the field of climate change and disaster risk 
management. It has collaborated with Kyrgyzstan since 1993 and a country office was opened in 
2009. The country office currently manages 18 projects in the country. It has also been 
providing technical support to investments from other agencies, such as the World Bank and the 
International Fund for Agricultural Development.  
57. 
In addition to FAO, there are three more EEs, namely SAEPF, ARIS, and RKDF. The SAEPF 
is the National Designated Authority, implementing policy related to environmental protection 
and the sustainable use of natural resources. It has a track record of managing a number of 
projects, including a forest management project funded by external donors. ARIS is a non-
commercial organization investing local priority issues and will implement specific activities of 
this project. The RKDF has funded 925 enterprises for a total amount over USD 275 million as of 
April 2018. It will provide a credit line via five commercial banks in this project. It also has 
experience with the International Fund for Agricultural Development in providing loans to 
agriculture producers and processers. All EEs are providing co-financing by way of in-kind 
finance or loans. There is a need for effective coordination among the AE and EEs.  
4.3.3. 
Programme-specific execution risks (medium) 
 
58. 
The funding proposal points out that the existing regulatory framework fails to support 
sustainable natural resources management, including the sustainable impact of carbon 
sequestration. For example, there is a lack of legal framework in carbon pricing initiatives, 
carbon emission reduction rights, ownership and retirement in the country. As the key returns 
of project investment are represented by carbon sequestration, this may make the project 
investment unattractive for investors. To mitigate the risk, the project will mainstream climate 
change and effective natural resources management at the policy level.  
59. 
At the investment level, it will invest in non-commercial tree species in state-owned 
forest areas where forests are established and maintained in order to ensure ecosystem services 
without specific direct economic purposes. The project will also provide incentives for forest 
protection via the provision of alternative livelihoods. Given the growth in rural energy

=== PAGE 119 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 119 
 
 
consumption and urban construction needs, the government’s commitment to prevent illegal 
logging in state-owned forests and ensure monitoring and surveillance on its investment will be 
critical to sustain the project impact. 
60. 
The project will try to attract private sector investment in forestry and rangeland 
protection activities. It will enable private sector engagement by enhancing the inclusive policy-
level dialogue and establishing stakeholder engagement process. However, 
afforestation/reforestation activities are unattractive for private sector participation: most of 
the non-commercial local varieties in forestry investments generate no or minimal financial 
returns but do generate ecological co-benefits at a 20-year horizon. As for rangeland 
investments activities, improvement of rangelands conditions will contribute over 70 per cent 
of the carbon sequestration potential of the project. However, rangelands are the centre of the 
livelihoods of rural communities in the target areas, thus, it needs incentives to change the 
behaviour of the private sector to invest in the activities that reduce the pressure of using 
rangelands in livelihood activities. To attract private sector investment in both forestry and 
rangeland protection activities, the project will provide different levels of concessionality 
depending on the return profiles of the investment. RKDF provides concessional co-financing 
for these activities. 
61. 
The AE has identified the risk that expected disbursement rates by RKDF and partner 
banks may not be reached due to the saturation of the agriculture lending market. According to 
the International Monetary Fund report, the agriculture sector accounts for the top second (18.5 
per cent) of the credit portfolio of banks and the first sector (28.3 percent) of microfinance 
organization. The national bank also encourages commercial banks to participate in the state 
programmes related to financing agriculture. The AE stated that the project in partnership with 
the national bank will explore new opportunities in agriculture lending for product 
development in cooperation with interested financial institutions. The AE responded that the 
capacity-building component could also serve as an incentive for the banks to reach the project 
target groups.  
62. 
Financial and economic analysis: the project provides disaggregated accounting of the 
financial and economic returns by type of activity and by component. While the financial returns 
in afforestation/reforestation are negative in 10 years and barely positive (financial internal 
rate of return of 11.1 per cent) in 20 years. The economic returns are expected to be higher 
(economic internal rate of return of 71.3 per cent) as it takes into account the valuation of 
carbon sequestration (USD 40/tCO2eq) and the ecosystem benefits. It also provides sensitivity 
analysis considering variations in climate change affecting costs and benefits, changes in 
potential sequestration and valuation, and also in implementation. Overall, the sensitivity 
analysis estimates economic internal rates of return ranging from 27.1 to 112.4 per cent under 
different scenarios. It is highly sensitive to the one to two-year delay in project activities, which 
emphasizes the importance of the timeliness implementation of the initial phase of the project.  
4.3.4. 
GCF portfolio concentration risk (low) 
63. 
In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on GCF portfolio concentration in 
terms of results area and single proposal is immaterial.  
4.3.5. 
Compliance (medium)  
64. 
In reviewing the activities contemplated under the project, it does not appear that the 
activities to be undertaken pose a high risk for money-laundering or financing terrorism. The 
AE has reaffirmed that it will apply its internal anti-money-laundering/countering the financing 
of terrorism policies to the project as required by the accreditation master agreement (AMA). 
Based on the information provided, Compliance assigns a medium compliance risk to the 
project.

MAFIM, RKDF and FAO will be responsible for overall management, supervision, guidance and 
technical support. 
69.

for the funds transferred to SAEPF and ARIS will be performed by them in accordance with 
terms, conditions, formats and requirements of FAO and the signed operational partners 
agreement. The administration by the operational partners of the funds received from FAO shall 
be carried out under its own financial regulations, rules and procedures, which shall provide 
adequate controls to ensure that the funds received are properly administered and expended. 
The operational partners shall maintain the account in accordance with generally accepted 
accounting standards. 
70. 
Procurements under the project will be done in accordance with the rules and 
regulations of FAO. 
4.5 
Results monitoring and reporting

=== PAGE 121 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 121 
 
 
71. 
The project is a cross-cutting project with mitigation and adaptation benefits related to 
CO2 sequestration, most vulnerable people and communities, and improved resilience of 
ecosystems and ecosystem services. The logframe fully reflects the fund-level impact and 
outcomes. In the project outcomes section both qualitative and quantitative indicators are 
included allowing for monitoring for the changes and transformations occurring as a result of 
the project. 
72. 
The implementation table in Annex 7 has been revised and includes the project

73. 
The Theory of Change description has been revised and is now clearly articulated and
