the project will overcome those barriers, bring transformational change and move towards the 
overarching project goal. 
4.6 
Legal assessment 
74. 
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 8 June 
2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.   
75. 
The proposed project will be implemented in Kyrgyz Republic, country in which GCF is 
not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not 
protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further 
assessed. The Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the 
government of Kyrgyzstan on 11 September 2018.  
76. 
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities. 
77. 
In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made 
subject to the following conditions:  
(a) 
Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the 
Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and  
(b) 
Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

=== PAGE 122 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 122 
 
 
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP116 
Proposal name: 
Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and 
Rangelands (CS-FOR) in the Kyrgyz Republic 
Accredited entity: 
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) 
Project/programme size: 
Small 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
 
    Scale: High 
1.1.1. 
General background 
1. 
Kyrgyzstan has been classified as a low-income, food-deficit country. Two-thirds of the 
country’s 6 million people live in rural areas of the country. Agriculture is the main source of 
livelihood, with livestock the leading source of income and primary source of nutrition; it also 
provides a financial safety net for the rural poor. Crop production has not been thriving well in 
the country due to a combination of inadequate irrigation infrastructure and the negative 
impact on productivity of worsening climate conditions. As fully articulated in the funding 
proposal and some of the annexes, poor management of resources and negative climate change 
impacts are causing overexploitation and overgrazing of large groups of unproductive livestock, 
increasing forest degradation and unsustainable harvesting of timber from mountain slopes. 
The interactions between changing weather patterns, the impact of these changes on 
communities and forests, and the degradation of natural resource systems due to human 
activities has led to an increase in damage from extreme weather events and a growing threat to 
the future provisioning of key ecosystem services. The Government of Kyrgyzstan recognizes 
the need to take action to halt this trend, as is evident from the key interventions for 
investments in rangeland and forest management, which are well elucidated in the country’s 
intended nationally determined contribution (INDC). The interventions will commit the country 
to reducing, by 2050, its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 35 to 37 per cent with 
international assistance. The proposed project has been designed to assist the country in 
meeting these commitments. The sequestration objective, which is well entrenched in the 
proposed strategy of the project, capitalizes on important co-benefits from adaptation and 
disaster risk reduction and is expected to be achieved through the following project 
components: 
(a) 
Component 1: evidence-based strengthening of natural resources management (NRM) 
governance. This component will support the Government’s ongoing efforts to 
harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks, and strengthen its planning, 
monitoring and evaluation systems; 
(b) 
Component 2: green investments for forests and rangeland rehabilitation. This 
component will introduce a process of local integrated rangeland and forestry resource 
planning built around forest, rangeland and livestock management practices. This will 
sequester carbon and is expected to be responsive to observed changes in the climate 
and enable the diversification of household income earning activities; and 
(c) 
Component 3: climate-sensitive value chains development. This will facilitate market 
opportunities that provide the financial incentives, funded by the Russian Kyrgyz

=== PAGE 123 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 123 
 
 
Development Fund (RKDF), for resource users to adopt and maintain management 
practices that sequester and preserve stored carbon. 
2. 
The targeted project areas were selected due to their high level of exposure and 
sensitivity to climate change stressors, mitigation potential, human poverty and dependency on 
natural resources. 
3. 
The total fund proposed to implement the project is USD 49.99 million. This total 
investment is expected to be provided as follows: 
(a) 
Grant from GCF = USD 29.99 million; 
(b) 
Senior loan from RKDF = USD 15.00 million; 
(c) 
In-kind contribution from Ministry of Agriculture, Food Industry and Melioration 
(MAFIM) = USD 0.317 million; 
(d) 
In-kind contribution from State Agency for Environment Protection and Forestry 
(SAEPF) = USD 0.340 million; 
(e) 
In-kind contribution from Community Development and Investment Agency (ARIS) = 
USD 0.342 million; 
(f) 
In-kind contribution from the beneficiaries = USD 3.602 million; and  
(g) 
In-kind contribution from the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations 
(FAO) = USD 0.400 million. 
1.1.2. 
Climate change element 
4. 
Both the climate change mitigation and adaptation needs of the country are adequately 
justified in this project. First and foremost, reducing GHG emissions in all countries is a very 
important contribution to the contribution to fulfilling the goals of the Paris Agreement. 
Kyrgyzstan’s INDC under the Paris Agreement commits the country to reducing its GHG 
emissions by 35 to 37 per cent by 2050 through international assistance. Although the 
document did not produce clear strategies to achieve the envisaged GHG emission reductions, it 
emphasized that forestry and land use/land use changes appear to be among the most relevant 
sectors to target in the country to secure both adaptation and mitigation targets. In order to 
structure and enrich the mitigation target interventions, FAO, as requested by the Government 
of Kyrgyzstan, began the process of developing a proposal for the project “Carbon Sequestration 
through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands in Kyrgyzstan (CS-FOR)”. In developing 
this project, FAO ensured that the climate change mitigation needs of the country were 
adequately and sufficiently built into the project development by using inputs from climate 
change and climate rationale developed earlier for the country. Many of the inputs came from 
documents already submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change 
(UNFCCC), given that the inputs for the design of this project were taken from government 
sector planning documents, including those prepared as part of the national climate change 
mitigation and adaptation plans (e.g. the INDC, Third National Communication (TNC), etc.). This 
conclusion is also supported by the report included as annex 9 of the funding proposal, which is 
titled “Climate Change and Ecosystem Based NRM: Implications for the Project”. In this report it 
was stated that the goal of the project is to contribute to the development of a low carbon 
emission and climate-resilient economy. The project objectives, which were clearly elucidated 
in this report, include: 
(a) 
To intervene in key hot spots of target areas with adapted forest and pasture 
investments; and 
(b) 
To clearly transform management of pasture and forest resources at the national and 
local levels to ecosystem-based sustainable NRM by enhancing an integrated and

=== PAGE 124 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 124 
 
 
participatory approach, which is adaptive to climate change and responsive to the needs 
of local communities. 
5. 
The key expected targets and coverage of this project intervention include carbon 
sequestration through investment in rangeland and forest management. Achievement of the 
carbon sequestration results is expected to lead to the capitalization of important co-benefits 
from adaptation and disaster risk reduction by supporting the Government’s ongoing efforts to 
harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks, and strengthen its planning, monitoring and 
evaluation systems. Carbon sequestration will be achieved through the introduction by this 
project intervention of a process involving local integrated rangeland and forestry resource 
planning built around forest, rangeland and livestock management practices. The process is 
designed to be responsive to observed changes in the climate and is expected to enable the 
diversification of household income-earning activities as part of the process. This process will 
engender market opportunities that will provide financial incentives for resource users to adopt 
and maintain management practices that sequester and preserve stored carbon. The financial 
incentives will be funded by a co-financing senior loan from RKDF. Achievement of the project 
targets and coverage discussed above are well and strongly aligned with the following GCF 
performance measurement framework indicators: impact potential; country ownership; and 
needs of the recipients/beneficiaries. The alignment with the other metrics, including paradigm 
shift, sustainable development potential, and efficiency and effectiveness, can be described as 
strongly medium or weakly high for reasons that will be discussed later in this assessment.  
6. 
From the information provided in the funding proposal and some of the annexes, it can 
be concluded that the estimated target in terms of the carbon that will eventually be 
sequestered by the project intervention will be about 19.8 million tonnes of carbon dioxide 
equivalent (tCO2eq) and will be delivered by the following interventions:  
(a) 
The creation of legal and management enabling environments supported by an 
innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning and monitoring 
system; 
(b) 
Community-based investments in natural forest regeneration, sustainable forest 
management, afforestation and reforestation; 
(c) 
Rehabilitation of rangelands and prevention of further degradation; 
(d) 
Diversification of options for community livelihoods; and  
(e) 
Reduction of emission intensity per unit of animal protein. 
7. 
The carbon emissions reductions that will be delivered by the sequestration activities 
listed above have been accurately estimated using appropriate methodologies, which were well 
presented in annex 3(a) to the funding proposal titled “CS-FOR – Carbon Accounting”. It is also 
estimated that a total of 432,450 individuals, including 246,497 females, will be direct and 
indirect beneficiaries of this intervention. The approach used for this estimation, which is based 
on the RIMA II methodology of FAO, is found to be appropriate for the analysis, which formed 
the basis of the estimation of direct and indirect beneficiaries. The independent Technical 
Advisory Panel (TAP) therefore concludes that the number of beneficiaries as a core indicator of 
impacts is accurately estimated and calculation methodologies correctly provided.  
8. 
With regard to climate change vulnerability, the project design actually focused on a 
carefully selected locality. The chosen intervention areas were explicitly discussed in detail in 
chapter 1 of the feasibility study. Analyses of the study area, consisting of four districts of the 
country, related to the extent and status of rangeland and forest resources as well as the 
identification of changes in key climate parameters and related hazards; these were presented 
in annex 6(b) to the funding proposal titled “Project Atlas”. The four target districts are said to 
be the most vulnerable to climate-related stresses and hazards. In these four districts, green 
investment interventions for forest and rangeland rehabilitation and livestock management

=== PAGE 125 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 125 
 
 
practices will yield carbon sequestration with co-benefits in adaptive capacity and improved 
disaster risk reduction. While carbon sequestration will be delivered at the level of local regions, 
the global implications cannot be overemphasized. In addition, at the national level, the project 
will promote market-driven incentives (via component 3 and private sector involvement) to 
promote income diversification thus reducing pressure on natural resources. 
1.1.3. 
Time frame of results 
9. 
The critical results expected to be delivered by this project will include: carbon 
sequestration from improved forest and rangeland management; livelihood of people in forest 
productivity activities; and better livestock management activities. Adaptive capacity 
strengthening is also expected to be delivered by component 1 of this project, which will 
strengthen ongoing efforts of the Government of Kyrgyzstan to harmonize relevant policies and 
legal frameworks as well as strengthen its planning, monitoring and evaluation systems. All 
these critical results will be achieved through different levels of impacts during the period of 
project implementation. For example, while reinforcement of government policies in adaptive 
capacity strengthening will start to manifest during the early stages of the intervention, carbon 
sequestration delivery may start to occur during the latter part of implementation due to the 
longer time needed for forested vegetative growth to occur. 
1.1.4. 
Potential mitigation/adaptation impacts and degree of lock-in infrastructure 
10. 
This project intervention covers both mitigation and adaptation responses to climate 
change in Kyrgyzstan. Climate change mitigation and adaptation are priority areas clearly 
articulated in the INDC of Kyrgyzstan that was submitted to the UNFCCC secretariat in 2015. 
The country’s INDC emphasized the importance of mitigation and adaptation as priority areas 
for Kyrgyzstan, especially as they are linked to the risks to people’s livelihoods, the 
environment, the national economy, and achieving wider Sustainable Development Goals. 
National climate change policies and action plans have also been developed in Kyrgyzstan for 
various sectors of the country’s economy, including: emergency situations; biodiversity and 
forestry; agriculture; and water management.1 Climate change is also addressed in other 
national responses to international policy, including the United Nations Convention to Combat 
Desertification (UNCCD) and the United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity. The 
national action plan and its activity frameworks for implementing the UNCCD in the Kyrgyzstan 
for 2015−2020 have many highly relevant actions on land degradation that are directly linked 
to climate change adaptation measures for the agricultural and livestock sectors, and 
particularly for pasturelands. The national action plan highlights that strengthening the capacity 
for state management of land resources, effective land-use policies, and achievement of 
sustainable use of land resources will be necessary; it states that a principal condition is to 
maintain and increase the potential productivity of land while maintaining vital ecosystem 
functions of soil. While most of these policy documents stress adaptation intervention, 
mitigating intervention in the form of carbon sequestration was much emphasized in another 
national document, the “Third National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan”, a key emphasis 
of this project. Thus the information provided in the funding proposal and the feasibility study 
utilized much of the important information in these national documents to highlight the fact 
that the interventions foreseen by this project are the most feasible and suitable mitigation and 
adaptation options for Kyrgyzstan.  
11. 
The project is expected to yield an average carbon emission reduction through carbon 
sequestration of about 987,568 tCO2eq, which translates to about 19,751,354 tCO2eq over the 
20-year lifetime of the project. These reductions represent 7.6 per cent of the country’s total 
 
1 See “Priority Directions for Adaptation to Climate Change in the Kyrgyz Republic until 2017”, which was approved 
by Government Decree No. 549 on 2 October 2013.

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 126 
 
 
emissions, and 22.6 per cent of the agricultural share of emissions. In terms of reduction cost, if 
the total project cost of USD 49.99 million will deliver a total of about 19,751 million tCO2eq of 
carbon reduction, this translates to a reduction cost of USD 2.53/tCO2eq. The project will deliver 
benefits to 432,450 individuals, of which 246,497 are females. The total represents 8 per cent of 
the country’s population.  
12. 
The interventions have a critical learning effect that will bring positive outcomes to 
avoid lock-in systems and ensure climate-proof results, and that can be replicated for other 
flood-vulnerable populations not covered in the proposed intervention. Actions that will be 
undertaken in component 1, especially those that will build the capacity of the climate 
governance system, will strengthen government planning, monitoring and evaluation systems, 
ensuring that climate-proof results are obtained. 
13. 
The iTAP therefore concludes that the potential of this project intervention is high. 
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
 
 
    Scale: High 
1.2.1. 
Comprehensiveness  
14. 
In the absence of this project intervention, unsustainable grazing will continue, causing 
the expansion of pasture areas, and exacerbated by the continued use of traditional extensive 
grazing practices, with negative impacts on the protection of forest cover. This has been 
identified as a major constraint to the increase of carbon sequestration and storage in forests 
and rangelands in Kyrgyzstan. Livestock rearing is a longstanding tradition of the Kyrgyz 
people, and so it is not surprising that over the years there has been an increase in the country’s 
livestock population. Consequently, the degradation of forest cover in the country will likely 
continue in the absence of clear incentives for communities to preserve and expand forested 
areas and to limit and more intensively manage grazing lands.  
15. 
The proposed project aims to achieve this key goal through the introduction of an 
integrated, locally determined, ecosystem management approach for rangeland and forest 
resources. This will be introduced by developing the activities as profitable ventures to enhance 
the livelihood of beneficiaries at the same time. The measures that will be delivered by this 
project will enhance maintenance, increase forest cover, and improve the ecological status of 
rangelands, leading to greater carbon sequestration and storage, both in trees and pasture soils. 
Furthermore, beneficiaries in the target areas will learn important climate resilient farming 
techniques that will not only deliver profitable farming, but also contribute to the national 
carbon sequestration objectives and result in more a diversified and profitable agriculture for 
beneficiaries in targeted areas. 
16. 
The harmonization of national policies and strategies relating to forest and grassland 
management, which will be undertaken in component 1of the proposed project, will deliver an 
enabling environment that will enhance the success of the various interventions. It will also 
create a conducive policy and institutional environment that will greatly enhance the 
replicability of the project interventions in other regions of the country.  
17. 
The project design process will bring together key governmental agencies and 
coordination bodies and will ensure identification of clear roles by the stakeholders themselves. 
This process will encourage a high level of performance during project implementation, provide 
a sound basis for expanded use of the new approach to local resource management, and lead to 
greater carbon sequestration in the replication phases. 
18. 
A key component of the intervention that involves capacity-building for the 
implementing entity (IE) is the incorporation of project activities into the regular annual 
workplans of the IE. This will normalize these activities as part of the implementing agencies’

=== PAGE 127 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 127 
 
 
vision and approach to resource management nationwide, and thus establish a solid operational 
capacity that will be invaluable when the interventions are replicated in the other regions of the 
country. 
19. 
Given the fact that the assessment carried out to identify target areas for this project 
was a national review, areas where the interventions can be replicated were also identified. The 
IE thus has a rich database of sites where the resource management/carbon sequestration 
approaches delivered by this project can be replicated. 
20. 
The project will also develop the capacity of some beneficiaries who will engage in key 
value chains, specifically those for nuts and dried fruits as an agricultural business. The fact that 
the project will create profitable markets for their products will also enhance their interest in 
opening up new markets following the intervention. 
21. 
It can therefore be concluded that the objectives of the project are well aligned and 
respond to the proposed theory of change and value chain articulated in the funding proposal. 
1.2.2. 
Potential for knowledge and learning 
22. 
Knowledge management and capacity-building for key stakeholders represent 
innovative solutions that will be delivered by the proposed project. Project stakeholders will not 
only gain new capacities and knowledge about the different interventions, but they will also 
gain tools and skills that will support them to factor climate change into their decision-making 
processes and livelihood strategies. The project will therefore deliver a strong platform for 
knowledge enhancement and learning for stakeholders.  
23. 
The interventions are innovative for Kyrgyzstan, especially when the national efforts to 
mainstream climate change into the project’s key intervention is considered. The project will 
deliver various innovative interventions: capacity-building of stakeholders to mainstream 
climate change considerations into their normal activities; development of market mechanisms 
for their livelihood; and improved farming and animal husbandry, among others. It can 
therefore be concluded that the type of investment proposed will support innovative 
interventions in Kyrgyzstan. 
1.2.3. 
Enabling environment/sustainability of outcomes 
24. 
The plan described in the funding proposal is well structured, covering: identification of 
the regions and sites where the chosen interventions will be carried out; development of an 
enabling environment for the governance and management of the selected interventions; and 
development of an enabling environment for the sustainable development and replication of the 
outcome. The feasibility study (annex 2 of the funding proposal) also describes these features of 
the project. It can also be argued that the use of grant funding from GCF, blended with co-
financing in the form of a senior loan, will not only will provide funds for the private sector and 
other beneficiaries whose livelihoods in sustainable agriculture will be enhanced, but it can also 
offer a very robust financial exit strategy. 
25. 
As presented in the funding proposal and its annexes, the project is planned to bring 
about very explicit behavioural changes in institutions, communities and groups. Component 1 
has been designed to strengthen the Government’s ongoing efforts to harmonize relevant 
policies and legal frameworks, and to strengthen its planning, monitoring and evaluation 
systems. This strategy is based on the awareness that a key measure in preserving the natural 
resources of the forest and rangeland ecosystem in Kyrgyzstan is to improve governance related 
to their management. Improved management will prevent livestock overgrazing, overharvesting 
of fuelwood and timber, will create an enabling environment that stimulates innovative 
practices and investments, and conserve and regenerate these resources.

=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 128 
 
 
26. 
It has been recognized that to achieve these goals there will need to be a change in the 
outlook of existing institutional management and the regulation of forest and rangelands. The 
funding proposal explained that the intervention will create a conducive enabling environment 
at existing institutions by supporting and harmonizing the regulatory framework for proper 
management and use of forests and rangeland resources. Not only will the capacity of existing 
institutions be repositioned to utilize modern NRM principles, but the capacity of their staff to 
understand climate change risks and NRM will be improved together with their skills and 
capacity to monitor and regulate the use of forest and rangeland. The project design is therefore 
intended to bring about behavioural change at institutions currently responsible for the 
management and regulation of forest and rangeland activities. This is expected to address the 
fragmented nature of the existing policy and regulatory environment  
27. 
The project design and implementation plans also cover behavioural changes within 
community groups and at the individual level through training and capacity-building activities. 
Some of the key capacity-building and training that will be delivered by this project and will 
lead to behavioural change in groups and relevant individuals with specific responsibility for 
activities that impact the ecosystems of forests and rangeland include: 
(a) 
Capacity development and mobilization of an expert group for technical assistance. The 
project will select personnel from local research and outreach organizations who will be 
trained on policy-making and management of natural resources. The expert group will 
include a gender and social development specialist, and the terms of reference for each 
member will ensure gender mainstreaming. Key activities for which the capacity of the 
expert group will be built and strengthened by the project will include: preparing 
communication materials and organizing information awareness campaigns to mobilize 
national stakeholders; organizing international conferences and meetings to sensitize 
stakeholders; organizing training sessions and workshops on forest and rangeland 
tenure arrangements, policy-making, and management of natural resources; proposing 
recommendations for enforcement of sustainable management and use of forest and 
rangeland ecosystems through participatory processes; identifying approaches for 
national stakeholder involvement processes; organizing a national stakeholders’ 
platform for policy dialogue for the management and use of municipal forest; and 
facilitating thematic workshops, and submitting the recommendation document to 
parliament; and 
(b) 
The capacities of monitoring groups at the central level of the State Agency for 
Environmental Protection and Forestry (SAEPF) and ARIS (the executing entities (EEs) 
for the project with FAO) will be built and/or strengthened by this project intervention. 
Key outputs of the capacity-building activities included in component 1 of this project 
will include enhanced capacities on: climate change risks and natural resource 
assessments; monitoring and evaluation with evidence-based integrated NRM planning, 
monitoring and evaluation tools and methodologies; facilitating linkages across 
evidence, primary data, information systems and the forest-rangeland ecosystem 
planning processes; and using a geo-referencing and evidence-based approach.  
28. 
The iTAP can therefore conclude that behavioural changes in institutions, communities, 
and individuals were fully considered and transparently presented in the funding proposal and 
its annexes. 
1.2.4. 
Replicability and scalability/knowledge and learning 
29. 
The potential for replicability and scaling up of the successes of this project intervention 
is presented in the funding proposal and annexes and can be summarized as follows: 
(a) 
The harmonization of national policies and strategies, which will be carried out as part 
of component 1 of the project, will not only benefit sound project delivery in the target

=== PAGE 129 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 129 
 
 
areas but will also deliver a more robust policy and strategy framework at a national 
level. This will create a much more conducive policy and institutional environment 
leading to more effective replication and scalability of the successful project activities 
and strategies. This will greatly improve the efficiency with which expanded actions can 
be undertaken in the future;  
(b) 
A key achievement of the project implementation strategy for CS-FOR is to bring 
together relevant government agencies and bodies responsible for management of 
forests, rangelands and animal husbandry in Kyrgyzstan. This achievement will enable 
clear delineation of the roles of agencies in the ecosystem management process, which 
will not only lead to a high level of performance for the current project but will also have 
a positive impact on its replication and scalability in the future in other regions. This will 
provide strong motivation for continued and expanded use of the new approach to local 
resource management leading to greater carbon sequestration; and 
(c) 
There is ample evidence in the description of the project design, given in the funding 
proposal and its annexes, that emphasis will be placed not only on proper monitoring of 
the outcomes but also on their comprehensive documentation using various tools, 
including geographic information systems. This will deliver a robust knowledge base 
that can be shared by others within and outside Kyrgyzstan who may want to replicate 
the ideas in the future. 
30. 
Through trainings, workshops and other knowledge-sharing activities built into the 
project design and delivery mechanism, and the monitoring, reporting and verification 
protocols that will be practised as part of this project, the proposal provides a clear framework 
to share knowledge and contribute to replication and/or scaling up. 
31. 
The project design and implementation strategies as presented in the funding proposal 
and some of its annexes, indicate that throughout the project implementation, government 
agencies and especially its policy and regulatory framework will participate in the delivery of 
the project activities. For example, SAEPF, a state agency with responsibility for regulatory and 
policy settings for forestry, rangeland and livestock management, will serve as the leading 
implementing agency for this project. The participation of SAEPF in that capacity and the 
mobilization of other relevant stakeholders will ensure that the climate change adaptation and 
mitigation measures to be delivered by this project will be mainstreamed into policies, laws, 
sectoral and national strategies and decision-making processes in Kyrgyzstan. 
32. 
Given the discussions above, the iTAP is of the opinion that the CS-FOR project as 
presented in the funding proposal and its annexes has a high potential to deliver a paradigm 
shift in the way forests, rangelands and livestock are managed in Kyrgyzstan. 
1.3 
Sustainable development potential  
 
 
    Scale: High 
1.3.1. 
Environmental co-benefits: 
33. 
Apart from contributing to Kyrgyzstan’s shift to low-emission sustainable development 
pathways through carbon sequestration, some of the project’s expected environmental co-
benefits related to afforestation/reforestation and forest enrichment will include: 
(a) 
Improved soil fertility and stability; 
(b) 
Reduction of land degradation; 
(c) 
Improvement in water retention in soils; 
(d) 
Provision of additional habitat to beneficial animals, such as pollinators; and

=== PAGE 130 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 130 
 
 
(e) 
Enrichment of biodiversity in the country’s ecosystem, especially in the areas where the 
project will be implemented. 
34. 
Another environmental co-benefit discussed in the funding proposal will result from 
pasture rotation, another project intervention, which is expected to improve plant diversity by 
releasing the growth potential of species that are otherwise suppressed under continuous heavy 
grazing pressure.  
35. 
The project plan has methodologies in place to measure and monitor delivery of the 
expected outputs. Most of the environmental co-benefits that are expected to be delivered by 
the project are actually components of improved resilience of the forest ecosystem. According to 
the funding proposal, this has been identified as one of the metrics to be monitored and 
methods were presented in the funding proposal such as geo-referenced monitoring and 
evaluation archives and reports that will be prepared regularly, demonstrating positive 
environmental outputs of this project. 
1.3.2. 
Economic/social co-benefits: 
36. 
Key economic co-benefits that will be delivered by this project can be summarized as 
follows: 
(a) 
The income of rural dwellers in the regions covered by this project is expected to 
increase through the investment provided. Specific financial benefits will come from 
better herd management, contributing to an expected rise of livestock production; 
(b) 
Agricultural productivity will also be enhanced leading to increased income for families 
that will participate under project funding related to ecosystem services that include 
fruit trees/nut plantations, supplying fruits and/or nuts and other commercial crops 
covered by the project to the selected value chains adopting green technologies; 
(c) 
Forest users will benefit from collecting non-timber forest products through 
certification according to the international voluntary standards for sustainable forest 
use and management; and 
(d) 
Economic and financial analysis carried out on the project and reported in annex 3 of 
the funding proposal showed that the project’s investment can generate up to 3,300 new 
full-time equivalent jobs. 
37. 
Social co-benefits that are expected to be delivered by the proposed project will include 
the following: 
(a) 
Community mobilization will occur in 50 municipalities of the project regions involving 
social mobilization, including gender training and institutional support; 
(b) 
The community mobilization will also lead to formation of task forces that will deliver 
important social duties such as fire management teams at community levels; 
(c) 
Women and youth (both young women and young men) will benefit from their specific 
inclusion in project activities, as outlined in the gender and social inclusion action plan 
(GAP); 
(d) 
Women and youth engagement in business opportunities in the project-supported value 
chains, through proactive inclusion in business related capacity-building activities; 
(e) 
High priority will be given to women’s participation in the value chain, especially when 
selecting the value chain; and 
(f) 
The project will also provide a platform for women to access improved natural 
resources (forests and rangelands) more directly, thus ensuring equitable distribution 
of benefits.

38. 
The project will therefore provide increased linkages among economic and social actors 
in the selected project regions. 
39. 
Further potential for enhancing sustainable development through the project 
intervention can be summarized as follows: 
(a) 
The project will contribute to increasing the country’s low emission and climate-
resilient productivity through carbon sequestration in its forests, rangelands and 
through better management of its livestock; and 
(b) 
 
(c) 
The project when implemented is expected to reduce ecosystem losses and induce 
financial benefits to beneficiaries in the target regions. These benefits are expected to 
percolate to other regions of the country by replicating the lessons learned. 
40. 
The iTAP ranked the sustainable development potential of this project intervention in 
Kyrgyzstan as “high”. 
1.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
 
Scale: High 
1.4.1. 
Economic and social needs and climate vulnerability 
41. 
According to information presented in the funding proposal and its annexes and from 
open literature sources, it is clear that the geography and topography of Kyrgyzstan render it 
highly susceptible to hazards and disasters, which are becoming more frequent due to climate 
change. These hazards and disasters include: drought, land and mudslides, avalanches, violent 
winds, downpours, ice and frosts, breakthrough of glacial lakes, floods, river erosion, and 
earthquakes. A large proportion of the Kyrgyzstan population lives in the valleys and foothills of 
the mountains, where vulnerability to these events is particularly high and losses are extremely 
high.  
42. 
Target beneficiaries and their productive assets in the project’s target regions, which are 
those deemed to be the most affected, are exposed to these risks. It has also been established by 
some of the studies on which the intervention strategies were based that many of these 
disasters, and especially their effects, are exacerbated by unsustainable management of forests, 
rangelands and livestock in the country. The proposed project therefore aims at delivering more 
robust resource management for forests, rangelands and livestock to reduce the negative effects 
of these disasters. It can therefore be concluded that given the economic, social and climate 
vulnerability the project interventions are necessary in Kyrgyzstan.  
1.4.2. 
Financial needs 
43. 
Kyrgyzstan is a low-income food deficit country with a population of nearly six million, 
of which two-thirds live in rural areas. Agriculture is one of the key economic sectors in 
Kyrgyzstan, with 48 per cent of the labour force dedicated to this sector, which represents 
almost 15 per cent of gross domestic product. As a result, the country does not have the national 
financial capacity to address the problems targeted by this project from its own resources due 
to the limited nature of national and local resources.  
44. 
The kinds of interventions required cannot attract funding from the very weak domestic 
private finance entities, nor can they attract financing from international sources, as returns are 
low and the payback period is indefinite for such investment in many cases, especially where 
the intervention will not deliver revenue streams. GCF grant funding is therefore needed to 
overcome the financial barriers. The grant funding from GCF will also strengthen the funds from 
RKDF, a private sector entity that will provide its contributions as wholesale credits to local

=== PAGE 132 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 132 
 
 
banks that will on-lend to entrepreneurs involved in the ecosystem enhancing intervention. The 
GCF funding is also expected to incentivize local entrepreneurs who will be involved in 
ecosystem businesses and who are also expected to contribute some level of investment equity.  
1.4.3. 
Institutional needs 
45. 
According to information presented in the funding proposal, institutions in Kyrgyzstan 
involved with management, regulation and policy setting for forests, rangelands and livestock 
lack the strong integrated policy and legal framework needed to deliver public-private 
partnerships in integrated natural resources management. Consequently, the capacities of 
institutions and of other relevant stakeholders will be built and/or strengthened by this 
intervention. For government institutions, areas that will be strengthened will include: policy 
and regulatory capabilities, especially targeted at forest, rangeland and livestock management; 
monitoring, reporting and verification capabilities for ecosystem-based interventions; low-
carbon production systems in forestry and agriculture, including livestock sectors, of the 
Kyrgyzstan economy. For non-government stakeholders, the areas of strengthening will range 
from ecosystem-based entrepreneurial activities, especially those that will deliver carbon 
sequestration opportunities and climate resilient livelihoods, to the financing of such activities.  
46. 
The iTAP ranked the potential for this proposed project intervention to meet the needs 
of the recipients to be high. 
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
 
    Scale: High 
1.5.1. 
Alignment with climate change policies 
47. 
Climate change policies: the proposed project is well aligned with the key climate 
change policies of Kyrgyzstan, as evidenced by: 
(a) 
The Climate Change Adaptation Programme and Action Plan for 2015−2017 for the 
forest and biodiversity sector, which includes the Government’s plans for ecosystem-
based adaptation; 
(b) 
The country’s INDC, which was submitted to the UNFCCC secretariat in September 2017. 
Mitigation and adaptation actions in the forest and rangeland management sector are 
included in the INDC; 
(c) 
The Climate Change Coordination Commission (CCCC), headed by the First Vice Prime 
Minister of Kyrgyzstan, which coordinates all activities in Kyrgyzstan related to climate 
change. SAEPF, the lead climate change institution in the country is the secretariat for 
CCCC. Since SAEPF is also an EE for this CS-FOR project, SAEPF is a member of the CS-
FOR steering committee and the project management unit for the project. This ensures 
an alignment of the CS-FOR project design and implementation with the climate policies 
of Kyrgyzstan; and 
(d) 
It is well articulated in the funding proposal that the project conforms to and is aligned 
with all national climate change strategies and action plans developed for various 
sectors, including emergency situations, biodiversity and forestry, and agriculture and 
water management. 
48. 
Other policies: the project is also well aligned with the national strategic development 
plan of Kyrgyzstan as contained in the following national documents: 
(a) 
National Strategy for Sustainable Development 2013−2017, which emphasizes the 
importance of climate change considerations as part of a sustainable development

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 133 
 
 
approach for the sustainable use of natural resources and sustainable economic growth; 
and 
(b) 
The national action plan and its activity frameworks for implementing the UNCCD 
Kyrgyzstan for 2015−2020 has many actions on land degradation that are highly 
relevant to climate change adaptation measures for the agricultural and livestock 
sectors, and particularly for pasturelands. 
49. 
Complementarity and coherence: coordination with ongoing and similar planned 
projects, including GCF projects, is ensured by two important factors: 
(a) 
The support that the United Nations Development Programme is providing to the 
Kyrgyz Government, which includes the Ministry of Emergency Situations, MAFIM, and 
SAEPF, in development of the National Policy for Adaptation to Climate ChangeFAO is 
also a partner in the process focusing on the forestry and agriculture sectors. The main 
findings and policy directions of this document are reflected in the CS-FOR proposal; 
and 
(b) 
A national adaptation plan (NAP) is currently being developed, with the United Nations 
Development Programme leading the process under the GCF readiness process. 
Elements of the ongoing NAP development are mainstreamed into the design of the 
project. FAO is a partner in the process and is supporting the country in preparing the 
NAP. Management of agriculture and forests is included among the already existing 
national strategies and will also be a priority under the NAP. 
1.5.2. 
Implementation capacity: accredited entity 
50. 
FAO has considerable experience and expertise and a proven comparative advantage in 
sustainable forest management, sustainable land management and climate change. It can 
therefore be concluded that FAO as the accredited entity (AE) is a good fit for the activities that 
will be carried out in the key sectors of the planned interventions. In addition, FAO has 
extensive experience in such interventions in Kyrgyzstan and the region. In particular, FAO has 
worked to develop management capacity for natural resources, including the assessment and 
systematic development of modernization plans for irrigation schemes, training on irrigation 
management transfer to improve performance, and tools and methods to improve water 
productivity in Kyrgyzstan and other countries of the region.. 
51. 
FAO has a very strong presence in Kyrgyzstan with field offices and adequate staff in the 
country to supervise the project. 
52. 
FAO has relevant experience in Kyrgyzstan, in terms of similar project funding in forest, 
rangeland, livestock, agriculture and water activities. Through its work in the past, FAO has 
experiences working with all the selected EEs for this project.  
1.5.3. 
Implementation capacity: executing entity 
53. 
The EEs for this proposed project are: SAEPF, ARIS and FAO. 
(a) 
SAEPF is the lead governmental agency for climate change in Kyrgyzstan. Apart from 
being the AE for GCF and the designated national authority for UNFCCC representing the 
country, it also serves as the Secretariat of the CCCC, a Kyrgyzstan national organization 
headed by the First Vice Prime Minister of Kyrgyzstan. CCCC coordinates all the 
activities in Kyrgyzstan related to climate change. The organizational mandate of SAEPF 
is therefore aligned with the project; 
(b) 
ARIS is Kyrgyzstan’s Community Development and Investment Agency and was 
established in October 2013 as a non-commercial organization. The mission of ARIS in

=== PAGE 134 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 134 
 
 
Kyrgyzstan is to provide support and assistance for sustainable poverty alleviation to 
local communities and local self-governmental bodies in resolving local social and 
economic problems by strengthening their capacity to determine and prioritize local 
issues, develop investment and action plans, and mobilize resources as well as to 
develop, implement and manage investments aimed at resolution of local priority issues. 
ARIS will mobilize the participation of communities in the proposed project. Its past 
experience and mandate are well aligned with the objectives of the proposed project; 
and 
(c) 
The considerable experience, expertise and comparative advantage of FAO in 
sustainable forest management, sustainable land management and climate change are 
highly suited to fulfil the role of EE for this project and to act as the overall coordinator 
as an AE to GCF. 
54. 
It can therefore be concluded that the organizational mandates of the three EEs are well 
aligned to their roles in this project.  
55. 
The organizational mandates and experiences of the EEs in related and similar projects 
in Kyrgyzstan demonstrate the capacity to manage the project, including procurement, 
coordination, environmental and social, and other specific and necessary activities to 
successfully execute the project. 
1.5.4. 
Ownership 
56. 
According to the structure of the project’s implementation plan described in the funding 
proposal and some of its annexes, decision-making responsibility for all the critical activities of 
the project lies with the country’s relevant institutions, which will rely on its domestic systems 
coupled with the international due diligence provided by FAO as the AE to ensure 
accountability.  
57. 
The participation level of non-national actors in the delivery of this project will come 
from FAO and RKDF. FAO, as the overall coordinator of the project and as an AE to GCF, will 
ensure that the project is implemented with international best practices while ensuring that the 
funds from GCF (and other financial support) are well utilized as designed. RKDF, the Russian-
Kyrgyz Development Fund, is the other non-national organization participating in this project. 
RKDF was established in late 2014 as an international organization per the agreements between 
the governments of Kyrgyzstan and the Russian Federation. RKDF will carry out a very 
important role in the execution of this project by providing a credit line through local 
commercial banks that already reach out to the project target areas. It can therefore be 
concluded that the level for non-national actors is minimized and is well justified in this funding 
proposal. 
1.5.5. 
Stakeholder consultations 
58. 
CS-FOR was developed and prepared following a request by the Government of 
Kyrgyzstan, and a no-objection letter was signed by the national designated authority (SAEPF). 
According to information included in the project proposal, the CS-FOR proposal was developed 
in consultation with stakeholders:  
(a) 
To ensure that the project design is appropriate and meets national and local needs; 
(b) 
To verify the feasibility of the activities included in the project components; and  
(c) 
To obtain feedback from all stakeholders on all aspects of the project, including the 
environmental and social management framework and its components (including 
grievance redress mechanisms and gender).

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 135 
 
 
59. 
The following pertinent information on stakeholders’ consultation was summarized 
from the funding proposal: 
(a) 
Stakeholder engagement was undertaken during the funding proposal development 
stage and will continue during project implementation; 
(b) 
Consultations during the funding proposal development stage were held through 
workshops with potential stakeholders, meetings with potential stakeholders, and 
structured consultations; 
(c) 
During project formulation missions, “non-structured” bilateral meetings were also held 
on both technical and project management/implementation issues; and 
(d) 
The following stakeholders were consulted: SAEPF; Ministry of Emergency Situations; 
Ministry of Economy; Agricultural Projects’ Implementation Unit and the Department of 
Pastures, Livestock and Fisheries under MAFIM; ARIS; Association of Pasture User 
Unions “Kyrgyz Jaiyty”; RKDF; local non-governmental organizations Rural Development 
Fund and CAMP Alatoo; Kyrgyz Scientific Research Institute for Livestock and Pasture; 
Kyrgyz Scientific Research Veterinary Institute; Climate Financing Secretariat; 
KyrgyzHydromet; State Design Institute for Land Management “Kyrgyzgiprozem”; other 
donor agencies; and civil society. 
60. 
According to information provided in the funding proposal, targeted district-level 
consultations involved over 40 stakeholders, including representatives of local self-
governments (ayil okmotu), forest enterprises (leskhoze), pasture committees, women’s 
councils and traditional councils of the elders. Beyond this, Annex 2 (sub-annex 2a and 2b) was 
included in the funding proposal that dealt with the GAP and gender assessment plan. Pertinent 
issues in these documents, which dealt with how women and vulnerable groups have been 
considered in the design of the project, can be summarized as follows: 
(a) 
The GAP was prepared to implement the gender strategy and to ensure inclusion of 
disadvantaged groups (poor families, single-parent headed households and youth); 
(b) 
The GAP document presented various plans to cater to the needs of women and other 
vulnerable groups in the project area. The pertinent issues addressed in the GAP 
document can be summarized as follows: 
(i) 
An Expert Group (EG) that will be involved in regular discussion of the project 
and its delivery will be formed as part of the project monitoring and evaluation 
process; 
(ii) 
The EG, which will draw membership from relevant stakeholders, will include 
gender specialists; 
(iii) 
At least 50 per cent of the EG members will be women. Stocktaking tasks (legal 
and policy review and analysis of policies and legislation) will pay attention to 
women’s access to natural resources, and will consult community groups 
representing the interests of women and local women leaders; 
(iv) 
Training of participants from targeted key institutions in Kyrgyzstan whose 
capacity will be built to make evidence-based decisions on climate change and 
natural resource management must include at least 30 per cent women; 
(v) 
The community landscape management group that will be formed as part of the 
project implementation process will include the representative of the women’s 
council as well as of the youth organizations in each subdistrict (ayil aimak). At 
least 30 per cent of the members should be women; 
(vi) 
As part of a component of the project targeting green investment under the 
forest and rangeland rehabilitation subcomponent, service providers who will

=== PAGE 136 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 136 
 
 
carry out this investment will be selected and trained. At least 30 per cent of 
these service providers will be women; and 
(vii) 
Throughout the project implementation, women’s access to and benefits from 
improved natural resources (forests and pasture) will be monitored. 
61. 
The GAP document, which is provided in the funding proposal as annex 2, contains 
references to how the stakeholders will be engaged. Following review by the iTAP, the GAP 
document is found to be adequate. 
62. 
Given the various discussions above, the iTAP considers the country ownership metrics 
of the proposed project to be “high”. 
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
 
    Scale: High 
1.6.1. 
Financial structure 
63. 
Of the total funding needed to implement this project (USD 49.99 million), USD 29.99 
million has been requested from GCF as grant funding. This funding request from GCF has been 
justified in the funding proposal according to the following reasons: 
(a) 
Grant funding requested is justified given the substantial expected impact in 
contribution to sequester about 19.8 MtCO2eq through forest and rangeland investment; 
and 
(b) 
In the absence of GCF funding, joint investment in rangeland rehabilitation such as 
planned in this project will result in limited or negative financial return of investment in 
forests. This will be due to the high cost of funds from other sources, when and if they 
can be mobilized, the short-term nature of such funds and the high transaction costs 
associated with mobilizing different stakeholders for joint investments in rangeland 
rehabilitation. This will not make the investment attractive for private sector funding. 
64. 
The project as conceptualized and presented in the funding proposal and its relevant 
annexes does not entail the risk of crowding-out private and other public investments. Success 
will instead produce a convincing argument that a conducive environment exists and should 
catalyse further private sector investments.  
1.6.2. 
Co-financing/leveraging 
65. 
According to the information included in the funding proposal, with a contribution of 
USD 29.99 million from GCF, the project will have a grant to co-financing ratio of 1:0.7. The co-
financing will total USD 20 million, of which: 77 per cent will be from RKDF; 18 per cent from 
beneficiaries; and about 2 per cent each from SAEPF, MAFIM and ARIS. FAO will also contribute 
USD 400,000 to support policy dialogue around evidence-based NRM governance. Given this 
information, it can be concluded that the project will leverage an adequate level of co-financing, 
as determined by project needs from the other partners. 
66. 
As stated in paragraph 64 above, the success of this project will produce a conducive 
environment in Kyrgyzstan that will catalyse further investment, particularly from the private 
sector, to finance similar investments in the future.  
1.6.3. 
Financial viability/best practices 
67. 
Based on credible assumptions, evidence given in the economic and financial feasibility 
analysis of the project (annex 3 of the funding proposal titled “CS-FOR Economic and Financial

=== PAGE 137 ===
GCF/B.24/02/Add.02 
Page 137 
 
 
Analysis”) provided the following metrics for the expected financial performance of the project 
for a 20-year analysis: 
(a) 
Expected internal rate of return = 71.3 per cent; and 
(b) 
Estimated net present value (E-NPV) = USD 353.7 million.  
68. 
Robust sensitivity analyses covering parameters such as increased cost of the project 
due to climate change impacts, and decrease of benefits due to climate change impacts, were 
carried out. The result of the analysis showed that variation of these parameters between 20 
and 30 per cent will still yield acceptable results with the highest decrease in the estimated net 
present value from the base case of about 52 per cent when the benefits due to climate change 
impacts decrease by about 30 per cent. Even for this scenario, the estimated net present value 
will be USD 170.7 million while the expected internal rate of return will be about 41.2 per cent. 
69. 
From the review of the plans reported in the funding proposal and its annexes, it can be 
concluded that the project incorporated best practices and available technologies in its design. 
Multiple options for each type of intervention proposed were assessed before finalization of the 
design, which has now been presented for implementation. 
70. 
Based on the discussions presented above, the iTAP concludes that the efficiency and 
effectiveness of the proposed CS-FOR project are “high”. 
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
71. 
The iTAP therefore recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

CS-FOR  
Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan 
1 
Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests 
and Rangelands (CS-FOR) in Kyrgyzstan 
Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan 
1.
A Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) was prepared to implement the gender strategy
and to ensure inclusion of disadvantaged groups (poor families, single-headed households and youth).
Table below presents specific actions in each project component with indicative budget as envisaged at
the proposal stage. Minimum 30 percent was set as a target in accordance with the FAO’s operational
work and budget at the country and regional levels is allocated to women-specific targeted interventions1. 
Female-headed households, unemployed, youth, disabled groups will be prioritized as a vulnerable
groups to be targeted in the project activities. All trainings will have post-training evaluation questionnaires
to track the usefulness of the trainings. Gender assessment and monitoring will help to track how the skills
are being adopted that were acquired in trainings, how knowledge and equipment was used by women to
improve their businesses.
2.
The budget for the Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) was calculated according to the
underlying principles:
a.
For activities related to capacity development / training / awareness campaigns: 30% of
the budget is considered gender sensitive. This is related to the project target of
women inclusion in such kind of activities.
b.
For activities involving policy dialogue / regulatory framework: 50% of the budget is
considered gender sensitive. This is due to the estimates on beneficiaries of project
interventions.
c.
For activities involving investment in rangeland and forestry (comp 2 – excluding
training): 50% of the budget is considered gender sensitive. This is due to the
estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.
d.
For activities related to investment in climate sensitive value chains (comp 3 –
excluding training): 10% of the budget is considered gender sensitive, considering the
estimated potential outreach of the project.
3.
On the basis of the final GAP, the Gender and Social Development Specialist each year prepares
an annual work plan and budget of GAP for submission to the Project Director.
4.
The GAP is in line with the overall project implementation plan and timeline, thus all activities are
incorporated into the relevant components of the project.
5.
Capacity development activities will be carried out not only by providing trainings for beneficiaries
(both women and men) but also ensuring access of women and female headed households to decision-
making processes, such as securing quotas (at least 30%) for memberships in CLMGs, close
collaboration with and promotion of gender sensitization among the local government and core district-
level institutions (PUUs, WUAs) responsible for the management of access to natural resources (water,
pastures, land and other resources). The project will ensure a specific approach for women when
partnering with financial institutions and working on financial education of women entrepreneurs, 
supporting the latter one VCD. Through these activities it is anticipated to work on sustainability of the
project interventions.
1 FAO. 2012. Minimum standards for Women Targeted Interventions. FAO Policy on Gender Equality. 
Gender documentation for FP116

6. 
GAP planning and implementation. At the onset of the project implementation, the EG, under the 
overall responsibility of PIU Project Manager, with the technical support of the Gender and Social 
Development Specialist, will organize a workshop to validate the proposed GAP and sensitize key 
stakeholders. This would be followed by briefings and capacity development activities on GAP for the 
project team, implementation partners and other relevant stakeholders (refresher training will be organized 
later on as and when appropriate). A gender focal point will be nominated for each implementing partner, 
who will coordinate all issues related to gender mainstreaming and social inclusion. Gender-responsive 
mobilization and communication channels will be developed to reach both women and men, including 
vulnerable groups (such as female-headed households, low income families, unemployed). On the basis 
of the final GAP, the Gender and Social Development Specialist each year prepares an annual work plan 
and budget of GAP for submission to the Project Director.  
7. 
Project Manager will be held accountable for gender mainstreaming and social inclusion of the 
project, technically supported by the Gender and Social development specialist. This specialist will 
coordinate all work on gender mainstreaming in collaboration with the gender focal points of partner 
institutions and other stakeholders, the FAO Lead Technical Officer (LTO) and other members of the 
project team. 
8. 
Implementation of GAP will be supported by relevant FAO gender equality tools, such as FAO 
Gender mainstreaming and human rights-based approach: guidelines for technical officers 2 and the 
VGGT technical guide: governing land for women and men3. In addition, to promote and share its best 
practices FAO developed policies, guidelines and valuable tools in gender-mainstreaming: 
- 
In 2012, FAO endorsed a Policy on Gender Equality, the goal of which is to achieve equality 
between women and men in sustainable agricultural production and rural development, 
with the aim of eliminating hunger and poverty4. 
- 
Regional Gender Equality Strategy for 2019–2022 represents a common vision of what 
FAO intends to achieve over the next four years in promoting gender equality and women’s 
empowerment through its interventions in the region, and how5

CS-FOR  
Gender Assessment 
 
1 
 
Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests 
and Rangelands (CS-FOR) in Kyrgyzstan 
 
Gender Assessment 
Overview of gender inequalities in rural areas of Kyrgyzstan 
 
1. 
FAO conducted a gender assessment in 2016, titled ‘National gender profile of agricultural and rural 
livelihoods: Kyrgyz Republic’1. The assessment has informed the formulation of this project, including this 
section, as it will inform as well its implementation.  
2. 
Prior to its independence in 1991, about three quarters of women of Kyrgyzstan were in the official 
labour force, including those who worked in the state and collective farms2. During the period of transition 
to the market economy Kyrgyz women lost much economic, social and political power. By 2002 the official 
employment rate for women decreased by half. The period also saw weakening and disappearance of 
public services available during the Soviet time for working women, such as daycare centers, 
kindergartens, extended maternity leave, and access to basic health care. The loss of such services meant 
that women’s shouldering of the responsibilities to care children and the elderly increased.  
3. 
The Kyrgyz Republic ranked 90 out of 159 countries on Gender Inequality Index (GII). GII reflects 
gender-based inequalities in three dimensions (reproductive health, empowerment, and economic 
activity), and the higher the GII value the more unequal the country from the gender point of view. 
Kyrgyzstan’s gender inequality, with GII score of 0.394, is higher than neighboring Tajikistan (0.322) and 
Uzbekistan (0.287). While Kyrgyzstan fares well in terms of political representation and education 
attainment, it lags behind the two countries on maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth rate (see Table 
3).  
Table 3: Gender Inequality Index of Kyrgyzstan and Selected ECA Countries

Source: Human Development Report 2016, UNDP 
4. Kyrgyzstan’s legal framework guarantees women’s equality. Its constitution mandates equality 
between women and men and prohibits gender-based discrimination. Civil, penal, labour and family 
codes guarantee equal rights for men and women. In 2008 the Law on State Guarantees of Equal 
Rights and Equal Opportunities for Men and Women was promulgated to ban gender discrimination 
in the public administration. The Government has also addressed gender equality in key policy 
measures, and in 2012 approved a comprehensive and forward-looking gender strategy, entitled 
‘Gender Equality Strategy to 2020’. The strategy was translated into the National Action Plan on 
Gender Equality for 2015-2017, which was approved in December 2015 and the latest, as of 2018-
2020, approved in November 2018. Ministry of Labor and Social Development (MLSD) is an entity 
                                                             
1 FAO, National Ggender Pprofile of Aagricultural and Rrural Llivelihoods: Kyrgyz Republic (Country gender assessment series), 
2016. Available at: http://www.fao.org/3/a-i5763e.pdf     
2 ADB, Country Gender Assessment: Kyrgyz Republic, 2005

=== PAGE 150 ===
CS-FOR  
Gender Assessment 
 
2 
 
that is responsible for the gender mainstreaming and monitoring the implementation of the National 
Action Plan on Gender Equality (GE NAP). The gender strategy and action plan highlight: (i) women’s 
economic empowerment; (ii) functional education for girls and women; (iii) improving women’s access 
to justice and eliminating discrimination; and (iv) gender parity in decision making and expanding 
women’s political participation (v) regulatory policy to ensure gender mainstreaming across the state 
agencies and the standardization of national gender statistics. The strategy and action plan pay 
special attention to improving rural women’s non-formal education in such areas as information and 
communication technology with a view to facilitating their better access to employment and business 
opportunities3 as well as engaging women in income-generating projects, training for women in 
nutrition education and agrotechnology.   
5. 
Despite the adequate legal framework and Government’s proclamations of gender equality, 
researchers and development practitioners point out that Kyrgyzstan faces high gender inequalities. 
Deep-rooted patriarchal attitudes and conventional beliefs of women’s roles and responsibilities in the 
family and society serve to perpetuate gender inequality. While women’s councils exist at the rural 
municipality level to address gender issues and support women, many are not strong. Some argues that 
gender inequalities are on the rise due to recent revival of Islam4. Economic empowerment of women is 
lagging behind as barriers to their equal employment opportunities and entrepreneurship development 
continue to remain. In 2015, the average wage of men was 1.3 times higher than that of the women 
(14,743 soms for men, compared to 11,125 soms for women) 5. While both men and women take 
agricultural loans from microfinance institutions (MFIs), women borrow smaller sums than men, and are 
more likely to borrow as a group6. A recent study revealed that men are usually the primary decision-
makers within the household on such matters as finance (saving, spending and taking loans), choice of 
crops and the sale of agriculture and livestock products while women have more agency over non-
economic decisions (such as child care) and some economic decisions related to the spheres of their 
influence (i.e. kitchen garden)7.  
6. 
Violence against women is widespread, and there are reported cases of domestic violence, bride 
kidnapping, trafficking, early marriages and physical abuse. In rural areas, in particular, bride kidnapping 
and early marriages remain a matter of great concern. In its 2014 review of Beijing Declaration and 
Platform of Action, the country reported a research finding that ‘60 percent of marriages in the countryside 
in mono-ethnic areas are accomplished through bride kidnapping, of which two thirds are performed 
without consent of the girl’8. According to the 2006 data cited in the same Beijing+20 National Review, 
over 12% of women in Kyrgyzstan get married before the minimum marriage age of 18. The incidence of 
such marriages is higher in rural areas (14.2%) than in urban areas (9.7%). Early marriages are more 
common among poor households (16.5%) than in rich families (9.1%). Although more updated information 
is not available, an increase in fertility rate among young women – 4.4 children per 1,000 women aged 
15-17 in 2006 to 7.7 children in 2012 – may indicate that early marriages are on the rise.   
7. 
According to a population and health survey in 2012, 27% of all households are headed by a 
woman9. Rural area records a lower percentage of FHHs (21%), compared to urban area (36%). Sex of 
the household head is not a strong factor to influence the poverty level of the family. The NSC data 
suggests that female-headed, one-parent families are not poorer than male equivalents, but among the 
two-parent families, poverty is higher if they are headed by a woman (see Table 3 below). Similarly, 
assessments found that FHHs in Kyrgyzstan are not more likely to be food insecure than male-headed 
households (MHHs)10. Although FHHs are slightly more likely to be severely insecure (10% of FHHs in 
comparison to 7% of MHHs), the reverse is true for moderately food insecure households (16% of MHHs

8. 
Although no data or comprehensive analysis is available on the situation of wives of migrant 
workers, some reports mention extra challenges such women face. Episodes include wives not directly 
receiving remittances, which are instead sent to the migrant’s mother or elder sister12. On the other hand, 
a large number of women migrate for work both to foreign countries, as well as inside Kyrgyzstan. 
According to 2016 data from Russia, nearly 40% of the Kyrgyz citizens among official migrants in the 
country were women 13. Compared to other Central Asian countries, where the migrants in Russia 
originate, Kyrgyzstan records the highest proportion of women among the migrants in Russia. As for 
internal migration, women outnumber men: 64% of inter-regional migrants in 2014 were women, mainly 
rural women migrating to urban areas 14. Women may migrate together with males or alone, and a 
significant proportion of Kyrgyz women migrants are the primary breadwinners15. It is pointed out that the 
massive out-migration Kyrgyzstan has been experiencing is ‘affecting gender norms in multiple and 
conflicting ways’16. While negative consequences, including those for women and girls, may be numerous, 
some argue that such a large social disruption, which makes previous social norms and mental models 
untenable, may bring about some positive changes to women, such as males’ taking more childcare 
responsibilities and women’s seeking more work outside the home 17.  
 
Women in Agriculture and Natural Resource Management 
 
9. 
Agriculture underscores the lives of the rural population as almost all adults engage in some work 
related to crop and livestock production. Rural women work in crop cultivation, livestock production, food 
processing and marketing. In terms of formal labour, agriculture is the largest sector of employment for 
both women and men. Around a third of all working women and men are employed in agriculture, 
according to the statistics18. It must be noted that the share of women employed in agriculture has 
increased considerably in recent years. While women’s share in the agriculture (including forestry and 
fisheries) was about 40% during the decade leading to 2012, it increased to 44.5% in 201419.  This may 
have been caused by the tendency of male labour’s increased entrance into other sectors and their out 
migration. It could be also possible that this reflects women’s limited employment opportunities in other 
sectors.  
10. 
Employment patterns between women and men in agriculture differ. Women are more likely to be 
self-employed, while men are more often engaged as hired (contracted) workers. According to the NSC 
data, 73.9% of all self-employed women worked in agriculture in 2014, compared to 58.4% of all self-
employed men20.  
11. 
Women’s equal rights to properties, including agricultural land, are legally protected. The Land Code 
(1999) and Law on Introduction of Land Code of the Kyrgyz Republic (#46, 1999) are the main documents 
                                                             
11 NSC data cited in FAO (2016).  
12 International Federation for Human Rights, Women and children from Kyrgyzstan affected by migration: An exacerbated

migrants was 574,194, of whom 351,121 (61.2%) were men.

CS-FOR  
Gender Assessment 
 
4 
 
regulating land relations and establishing the grounds for the commencement, procedure for the exercise 
and termination of land titles. The Law on Agricultural Land Management (2006) enable women to ‘obtain 
and register individual rights to land shares by dividing land plots in plots into individual parcels for 
independent management and transactions’21. The law recognizes men’s and women’s equal right to 
inherit land. Only 3 percent of rural women have sole ownership of land, whereas there are about 22 
percent of rural men who own land. About 60 percent of rural women do not own any land22 
12. 
Researchers and development practitioners argue, however, that exercising such rights is a 
challenge for women due to the prevailing notion that males are natural heads of the household. Land is 
usually registered under husband’s name. According to a 2011 country assessment of access to land, the 
formal law in Kyrgyzstan ‘has not in most cases been successful in protecting and improving women‘s 
rights, including rights to access and own land’23. For example, although women who leave her birth 
families to marry have the right to sell her land shares to the families, they seldom do so because such 
a demand is seen as shameful24. Women who are divorcing have similar challenges in claiming their land 
shares. It is pointed out that young wives, ex-wives, daughters-in-law and widows are ‘least likely to 
challenge patriarchal traditions about property rights’25.  
13. 
A Livelihood Study conducted during the project preparation organized focus group discussions in 
the project area, including those with women. The results revealed that women and women's groups are 
not considered as separate groups that have special needs, for which special approach is required. A 
major difficulty that women in the project area face is stereotypes and traditional rights that limit the 
participation of women in the management of natural resources. In communities, the public role of women 
is reduced to solving the problems of benefits and pensions, or to resolving social issues related to the 
upbringing of children and motherhood. Often women are not informed about meetings held on pasture 
and forest issues, so their interests are not taken into account. 
14. 
In spite of this, the participants of the discussion note that in recent years there has been 
opportunities for women and women's groups in solving various issues related to natural resources. 
Despite the fact that there are no women in the forestry and pasture committee, especially in the 
management body, women's committees often became involved in the discussion of problems in the use 
and management of natural resources. 
15. 
Livestock. Women’s engagement in animal husbandry is significant.  Women are particularly active 
in raising young animals, milking, and preparation and sales of dairy products. Women in households 
which graze animals in distant pastures during summer would travel with the husbands and children, 
maintain seasonal home in the pasture and carry out domestic tasks in addition to their responsibility in 
animal raising and other economic and livelihood activities of the family26. However, women’s participation 
in the livestock sector is considered secondary to males’ under social norms prevailing in rural areas which 
label livestock production primarily a male-led activity. Official data on individual ownership of livestock 
are unavailable. Studies found that FHHs are less likely to own livestock (41%) than male-headed 
households (56%), but there is no significant difference in terms of the number of livestock owned by each 
type of household27.  
16. 
Women’s involvement in pasture management is also limited. A recent study on gender and pasture 
management points out that traditionally women’s rights to pastures are secured through their male kin 
members - husbands or, if the family is headed by a woman, her male relative28. Women’s representation 
in the executive committees (Pasture Committees or PCs) of Pasture Users Unions (PUUs) is very low.
